## assistant

You’re right. **Conditional does not yet match Intro to LR’s structure closely enough.** I built the curriculum, but used a simpler interface.

| Intro to LR | Conditional now |
|---|---|
| Integrated sequence of notes, tools, checkpoints, drills, practice, and capstones | Lesson list with a shared practice screen |
| Learning-path rail visible while working | General section navigation |
| Previous/next actions and lesson contents | Recommended next step, longer lesson pages |
| Shared workspace with reference/tool drawer | Separate reference pages |
| Distinct guided learning experiences | Repeated lesson and question template |

**The assignment lock is shared by both bootcamps.** It is a separate account-access issue.

My recommendation is to preserve the conditional content and rebuild its presentation around Intro’s workspace and integrated action path: **learn → guided activity → skill drill → review → mixed application**, with your three difficulty levels and targeted assignments retained.

I overstated how closely the first release matched Intro. It needs this structural pass.

shouldnta havesequence o diffuculty it should allow them tthe stage again the curriculum according to where hte yare atw thehter e hteyaero conditional


## assistant

Yes. **The curriculum should start students where they are, rather than make everyone complete Beginner, then Medium, then Advanced.**

There should be two ways through it:

- **From the beginning:** a guided teaching sequence for someone new to conditionals.
- **From their current stage:** assign the exact skills and difficulty they need, skipping what they already know.

A student could need Beginner Group 3 language, Medium linking, and Advanced contraposition at the same time. Their curriculum should accommodate that uneven skill profile.

Difficulty should be selectable within each skill. The teaching sequence provides direction; it should not force every student through the same difficulty progression.

Understood. **You assign their starting point and activities. Students follow those assignments; they do not select a difficulty level.**

Levels 1–3 describe the demands of individual activities at different points in the curriculum. They are not three complete passes through the course.

You can assign the full curriculum from the beginning, start someone at a later stage, or assign one specific activity to address a gap. The curriculum’s order follows how conditional skills build on one another.

<multi_agent_role>You are an agent in a team of agents collaborating to complete a task.

You can spawn sub-agents to handle subtasks, and those sub-agents can spawn their own sub-agents. All agents in the team, including the agents that you can assign tasks to, are equally intelligent and capable, and have access to the same set of tools.

You can use `spawn_agent` to create a new agent, `followup_task` to give an existing agent a new task and trigger a turn, and `send_message` to pass a message to a running agent.
`send_message` calls may be read by a human, so ensure they are legible. Always put proper spaces between words and/or numbers.
Child agents can also spawn their own sub-agents.

When you provide a response in the final channel, that content is immediately delivered back to your parent agent.
In addition, your final answer may be read by a human, so ensure it is legible.

You will receive messages in the analysis channel in the form:
```
Message Type: NEW_TASK | MESSAGE | FINAL_ANSWER
Task name: <recipient>
Sender: <author>
Payload:
<payload text>
```
You may also see them addressed as to=/root/..., which indicates your identity is /root/...

Note that collaboration tools cannot be called from inside `functions.exec`. Call `spawn_agent`, `send_message`, `followup_task`, `wait_agent`, `interrupt_agent`, and `list_agents` only as direct tool calls using the recipient shown in their tool definitions, such as `to=functions.collaboration.spawn_agent`, since they are intentionally absent from the `functions.exec` `tools.*` namespace. Available tools in `functions.exec` are explicitly described with a `tools` namespace in the developer message.

All agents share the same directory. In detail:
- All agents have access to the same container and filesystem as you.
- All agents use the same current working directory.
- As a result, edits made by one agent are immediately visible to all other agents.

When calling `wait_agent`, prefer longer waits (minutes) to avoid busy polling.

There are 4 available concurrency slots, meaning that up to 4 agents can be active at once, including you.

Full-history forks (`fork_turns` omitted or `"all"`) inherit the parent model and reasoning effort and do not accept overrides. Only set `model` or `reasoning_effort` when explicitly requested by the user, applicable `AGENTS.md` instructions, or skill instructions; when doing so, set `fork_turns` to `"none"` or a positive integer string.</multi_agent_role>

While the provided sources do not detail every single LSAT Logical Reasoning question type (such as Parallel Reasoning or Method of Reasoning) they offer a comprehensive breakdown of the core activities required for analysis and specifically target the most critical error tested in Flaw questions. The breakdown of conditional logic primarily revolves around two main analytical activities: Chaining (deriving new rules) and Triggering (deriving new facts). I. General Breakdown Activity: Chaining Conditionals (Deriving New Rules). Chaining involves synthesizing multiple conditional rules to create a new inferred conditional statement. This is crucial for questions that require you to draw broad conclusions from a set of premises (often seen in Inference/Must Be True contexts). Connecting Rules: You must determine if the necessary condition (right side) of one conditional statement matches the sufficient condition (left side) of another. For instance if A rightarrow B and B rightarrow C they chain to infer a new conditional relationship: A rightarrow C. Bad Conditional Reasoning Example: The author is just bad at conditional reasoning. It’s sad really. Bad Conditional Reasoning occurs when the author reads the conditionals supplied in the premises incorrectly. 1. There are conditional premises. 2. Crazy person concludes something by reading the conditional premises backwards without negating. OR 1. There are conditional premises. 2. Crazy person concludes something by negating the conditional premises and reading it forwards. 2. Bad Causal R\\n\\n---\\n\\n## Source: Flaws.pdf.pdf\\n\\n**Path:** LSAT DUMP/LR/Assumption and Objection Family/Flaw/Notes/Flaws.pdf.pdf  **Modified:** 2025-07-18T02:45:07.591000+00:00  **Words:** 864  **Doc ID:** None\\n\\nFlaws 1. Necessary vs. Sufficient Presenting a conditional rule and then misusing it by either reversing or negating it improperly (illegal reversal or illegal negation). • *Example:* People who have seen *The 13th* have a great understanding of the US prison system. Since Megan hasn’t seen it, she must have a poor understanding of the US prison system. • *Example:* People who have seen *The 13th* have a great understanding of the US prison system. Jane understands the system well, so clearly sh\\n\\n---\\n\\n## Source: Conditional.gdoc\\n\\n**Path:** LSAT DUMP/LR/Conditional/Conditional.gdoc  **Modified:** 2025-10-17T08:42:29.714000+00:00  **Words:** 658  **Doc ID:** 10e6Okb__aaEYdU2T2gmAD95tnbTy4KYtrggk3Stl4sQ\\n\\nConditional logic forms the bedrock of many arguments and inferences tested on the LSAT requiring test-takers to demonstrate proficiency in recognizing the contrapositive logic and avoiding common illegal moves. While the provided sources do not detail every single LSAT Logical Reasoning question type (such as Parallel Reasoning or Method of Reasoning) they offer a comprehensive breakdown of the core activities required for analysis and specifically target the most critical error tested in Flaw questions. The breakdown of conditional logic primarily revolves around two main analytical activities: Chaining (deriving new rules) and Triggering (deriving new facts). I. General Breakdown Activity: Chaining Conditionals (Deriving New Rules). Chaining involves synthesizing multiple conditional rules to create a new inferred conditional statement. This is crucial for questions that require you to draw broad conclusions from a set of premises (often seen in Inference/Must Be True contexts). Connecting Rules: You must determine if the necessary condition (right side) of one conditional statement matches the sufficient condition (left side) of another. For instance if A rightarrow B and B rightarrow C they chain to infer a new conditional relationship: A rightarrow C. Contraposing Chains: You can contraposit an entire chain starting from the final necessary condition and negating back to the initial sufficient condition. If the chain is A rightarrow D the contrapositive is If not D then not A. The LSAT commonly rewards the ability to connect the beginning of the chain to the ending of the chain. Negating Complex Ideas: If the idea being contraposed (the trigger or the outcome) is a complex idea connected by and or or (e.g. A and B) you must remember to flip the truth values when negating. And turns into or and or turns into and. This ensures you correctly flip the truth value of everything in the complex idea. II. General Breakdown Activity: Triggering Conditionals (Deriving New Facts). Triggering is the process of using a known fact to activate a conditional rule and derive a certain factual inference. This is the process utilized in many Must Be True and Inference questions. A conditional rule (Sufficient rightarrow Necessary) can only be used in two valid scenarios: 1. Affirming the Sufficient Condition: If a fact confirms that the sufficient condition (the left side the trigger) is true then the necessary condition (the right side the outcome) must necessarily follow. 2. Denying the Necessary Condition (Contrapositive Logic): If a fact confirms that the necessary condition (the right side the outcome) is not true then the sufficient condition (the left side the trigger) cannot have been true. If the fact relates to the conditional rule in any other way (e.g. affirming the necessary or denying the sufficient) the rule just disappears and has no power over us. III. Breakdown in Specific Question Types and Contexts. Flaw Questions. Flaw questions are specifically designed to test whether you can recognize errors in conditional reasoning. The Conditional Flaw: The specific mistake frequently made by LSAT authors is confusing the necessary and the sufficient. This happens when an argument presents a rule (A rightarrow B) observes that the right side (the necessary condition B) is happening and then attempts to conclude that this proves the left side (the sufficient condition A) is happening. Breakdown Method: Your breakdown must identify the conditional rule and then recognize that the author has committed the illegal move of reading the conditional backward. This is the primary error the LSAT tries to ensure students avoid. For example learning that Alice is in the USA (necessary condition) does not allow you to conclude she is in NYC (sufficient condition). Inference and Must Be True Questions. These questions rely heavily on the ability to correctly apply the chaining and triggering activities described above. Breakdown Method: When presented with premises (rules) and facts you must diagram the conditionals and perform valid inferences. You must look for ways to chain rules together to find a connection from the premises to the answer choices.\\n\\n---\\n\\n## Source: Conditional Understanding.gdoc\\n\\n**Path:** LSAT DUMP/LR/Conditional/Conditional Understanding.gdoc  **Modified:** 2025-08-13T02:18:08.873000+00:00  **Words:** 603  **Doc ID:** 141zf_n8I7K0nB5Gmohu3hqAxJWgKVP2GRT6Xo7UpFrc\\n\\nFiguring Out The Language. Lets tackle the biggest challenge in conditional reasoning figuring out the language. Well start by returning to our first example and pointing out the target of each indicator word. The indicators target is the word or phrase it is referring to. If Im breathing then air is present. Each instance of breathing has air. When Im breathing air is present. Im breathing only if air is present. Any breathing means air is present. Only the presence of air allows for breathing. All instances of breathing have air present. Breathing depends on air being present. Every instance of breathing has air present. I need air to breathe. In order to breathe I must have air. I have to have air if Im breathing. People who breathe need air. Notice that in each of these examples the indicator is right before its target. This will be the case in the majority of conditional statements you read but acting on the generalization that the indicator is always immediately before its target will lead you into trouble. Air must be present for breathing. Air is essential for breathing. Air is necessary for breathing. Air is a precondition for breathing. Air is required for breathing. In these examples the target precedes the indicator. Why do they have to make it so difficult you ask? Because the LSAT wants you to understand what the sentence is really saying. They dont want you to see an indicator word and go on autopilot. You also cannot generalize that these specific necessary indicators will always come after their target. Each of them can switch sides. THE WHAT TEST. The way you can get around all this ambiguity is by taking the time to actually understand what the sentence is telling you. When you see an indicator and you have even a little doubt about its target ask yourself one question: What is the indicator referring to? The What Test is simple. You can perform it by adding a what to whichever side of the indicator makes grammatical sense. Heres how you perform the What Test: If what? When what? Any what? All what? Every what? In order to what? People who what? Each what? Must what? Whats necessary? Whats required? Only if what? Depends on what? Need what? Have to what? Whats essential? Whats the precondition? The What Test will probably not be necessary for the indicators like if and all because their target is clear. With some of the more confusing necessary indicators like depends and required you may use the What Test more. Air is required for breathing. I require air to breathe. The What Test probably seems simple to you and it is. However it is only simple because Im taking the split second to actually understand the sentence and figure out what require is referring to. You can do this too just be sure to take your time with conditionals in the beginning and youll be lightning quick in a couple weeks. Your Friend the Personalized If/Then. Personalized if/thens are translations for conditionals. Heres another safeguard on conditional diagramming. Make a habit of translating conditionals into personalized if/thens. Personalized if/thens require you to put yourself in the conditional statement and ask yourself what you know about your situation. This can get you out of confusion panic and back to the real world. HOW TO CREATE A PERSONALIZED IF/THEN. Imagine youre confused on what a conditional means. Instead of panicking: 1. Try to understand the casual non-conditional meaning of the sentence. 2. Take a guess at which half of the statement is the sufficient condition.\\n\\n---\\n\\n## Source: Conditional logic intro_.pdf\\n\\n**Path:** LSAT DUMP/LR/Conditional/Notes/Conditional logic intro_.pdf  **Modified:** 2024-09-23T20:04:05+00:00  **Words:** 580  **Doc ID:** None\\n\\nUnderstanding Conditional Logic and Contrapositives in LSAT Logical Reasoning Clarifying Conditional Logic Conditional reasoning, or \\"the art of the if/then,\\" is a fundamental concept on the LSAT that helps you understand logical relationships within arguments. Focus on finding Conditional Indicator words and using them as a way to organize how you write or think about the If-Then rule. Write or think through the contrapositive as well. Beware illegal Reversals or Negations. Conditional Logic by type Recap: Principle (the left side of the principle matches the evidence, the right side matches the conclusion). Parallel (diagramming these arguments with algebraic recipes allows us to be more picky and decisive when evaluating answers). Assumption (the conclusion gives us an A to Z logic path we want to prove, and the evidence + the correct answer choice assemble together to create that A to Z path). Must Be True / Most Supported (multiple conditionals often chain together, and provided facts often match the contrapositive-trigger of a conditional and allow us to infer the contrapositive-outcome). Flaw / Parallel Flaw (flaw + conditional logic usually means the necessary vs sufficient flaw using a rule in an illegal backwards or opposite fashion). instructions: Try these 10 questions in 22.5 minutes. Try to answer them all within your time limit, then circle back and confirm your thinking (untimed) before confirming your answers and reading the explanations, if available. CONDITIONAL KEYWORDS: Put on Left (sufficient conditions): if, when, any, each, all, every, the only, whenever, whoever. Put on Right (necessary conditions): only if, must, requires, ensures, always, implies, presupposes, depends on, guarantees. If-Not (negated version on left): unless, without, until, except. Negative to Positive conversions: No A are B = All A are not B. Not all A are B = Some A are not B. Few A are B = Most A are not B. Problems to Complete (10 total): PrepTest 130 Section 3 Question 13. PrepTest 130 Section 3 Question 14. PrepTest 131 Section 1 Question 16. PrepTest 131 Section 1 Question 19. PrepTest 134 Section 2 Question 3. PrepTest 134 Section 2 Question 22. PrepTest 134 Section 2 Question 23. PrepTest 135 Section 2 Question 15. PrepTest 125 Section 4 Question 23. PrepTest 125 Section 4 Question 25. Pause! For every mistake you made in this set, complete the sections below in as much detail as possible: Why did I eliminate the right answer? Why did I choose this wrong answer? What can I do next time to get this right? Additional notes.\\n\\n---\\n\\n## Source: intuition technique when stuck( conditional logic) .pdf\\n\\n**Path:** LSAT DUMP/LR/Conditional/Notes/intuition technique when stuck( conditional logic) .pdf  **Modified:** 2024-05-16T01:17:45+00:00  **Words:** 370  **Doc ID:** None\\n\\nHow to Figure Out Which Is Which: The What Test vs. Personalized If/Then The What Test The What Test is designed to resolve ambiguities in sentences with conditional indicators by pinpointing exactly what the indicator refers to. How to Perform the What Test This test involves adding a \\"what\\" to whichever side of the indicator makes grammatical sense, helping clarify the target of the condition. If what? People who what? Depends on what? When what? Each what? Need what? Any what? Must what? Have\\n\\n---\\n\\n## Source: intuition technique when stuck( conditional logic) .gdoc\\n\\n**Path:** LSAT DUMP/LR/Conditional/Notes/intuition technique when stuck( conditional logic) .gdoc  **Modified:** 2025-06-23T03:31:37.970000+00:00  **Words:** 370  **Doc ID:** 1ZthkbR3x5oHk9Rqv2ZOzR6j_tZ4HDsM45S_K0hBZlpA\\n\\nHow to Figure Out Which Is Which The What Test vs. Personalized If/Then. The What Test. The What Test is designed to resolve ambiguities in sentences with conditional indicators by pinpointing exactly what the indicator refers to. How to Perform the What Test. This test involves adding a what to whichever side of the indicator makes grammatical sense helping clarify the target of the condition. If what? People who what? Depends on what? When what? Each what? Need what? Any what? Must what? Have to what? All what? Whats necessary? Whats essential? Every what? Whats required? Whats the precondition? In order to what? Only (if) what? This test is typically unnecessary for straightforward indicators like if and all but proves invaluable for more confusing indicators such as depends and required. Example of The What Test. Sentence: Air is required for breathing. Implementation: Question: What is required in this sentence? Answer: Air is the thing that is required. Personalized If/Then Strategy. Personalized If/Then is a technique used to translate conditional statements into personal contexts simplifying understanding and ensuring correct logical diagramming. How to Create a Personalized If/Then. 1. Understand the casual non-conditional meaning of the sentence. 2. Take a guess at which half of the statement is the sufficient condition. 3. Put yourself in the situation of your guess and ask what you know about yourself. 4. If your guess is correct you will find that the necessary condition has to pertain. Example Translation into Personalized If/Then. Original Statement: Orchids depend on frequent conversation. Casual Meaning: Orchids need frequent conversation. Guess: Orchids as the sufficient condition. Personalization: If Im an orchid what do I know about me? I depend on frequent conversation. If Im an orchid then I have frequent conversation. This method not only aids in understanding the conditional but also verifies its correctness. Incorrect guesses can be easily identified and corrected through this personal context. Advantages of Personalized If/Then. Helps intuitively understand conditionals by imagining yourself as part of the scenario. Provides confidence throughout tests especially in correctly interpreting conditionals not phrased as if/then statements. Both the What Test and Personalized If/Then Strategy are crucial tools in deciphering and applying conditional logic particularly in competitive exams like the LSAT.\\n\\n---\\n\\n## Source: Conditional Chains.pdf\\n\\n**Path:** LSAT DUMP/LR/Conditional/Notes/Conditional Chains.pdf  **Modified:** 2024-07-02T20:46:11+00:00  **Words:** 352  **Doc ID:** None\\n\\nConditional Chains Conditional statements are like Legos. They are more interesting when combined. You can latch them together in predictable ways. Example Statement 1: If I buy an espresso machine, I will end up in the highly-caffeinated poor house. Statement 2: If I end up in the highly-caffeinated poor house, I will finally be cool enough to wear non-ironic plaid. Let’s think each of these statements through. Let’s place ourselves in this situation and see what we know. Instructions and Example. Ill give you a conditional statement. You will rephrase it into the simplest conditional phrasing if/then format. Example Statement: You cant master the LSAT without mastering conditional reasoning. Credited Answers: If you master the LSAT then you master conditional reasoning. If you dont master conditional reasoning you wont master the LSAT. Explanation: If you cant master X without Y Y is required in order to master X. That can be rephrased into if/then format in two ways: 1. If you master X then you have Y. 2. If you dont have Y you cant master X. Tasks: 1. Consider the conditional relationship at play in each statement. 2. Rephrase the statement using if/then format. Statements and Answers. 1. Sentence A policy change will occur only if the board approves it. Translation: Contrapositive: 2. Sentence Keeping plants healthy requires regular watering and sunlight. 3. Sentence No solution will be effective unless it addresses the root problem. 4. Sentence Whenever a fire alarm sounds the building must be evacuated immediately. 5. Sentence If the weather remains cold the lake will stay frozen. 6. Sentence Effective communication becomes necessary only if conflicts arise within a team. 7. Sentence No significant innovation can occur without prior experimentation. 8. Sentence If a country aims to improve literacy rates it requires substantial investment in education. 9. Sentence The machine will operate correctly unless the power supply is interrupted. 10. Sentence To achieve long-term success one must consistently adapt to market changes.\\n\\n---\\n\\n## Source: Conditional Quiz.gdoc\\n\\n**Path:** LSAT DUMP/LR/Conditional/Conditional Quiz.gdoc  **Modified:** 2025-08-13T02:37:40.188000+00:00  **Words:** 222  **Doc ID:** 1BR2ECD1l3vLQnCgfQuq4wck51JWv2bTPcy1bCfoHIe4\\n\\nWord Bank. 1 none without. 100 some until. present or and. 51 absent nor. text(await tools.exec_command({cmd:"python -c \"from pathlib import Path; from pypdf import PdfReader; root=Path('raw/sources/lsat-curriculum/_stragglers/recovered-ai-gather/Claude 2/02_PROJECTS/Curriculum/Curriculum/Conditional Logic/Notes'); names=['Conditional logic intro_.pdf','LSAT Logical Reasoning_ Contrapositives and Conditionals( 6 step process).pdf','Group 2 breakdown .pdf','Group 3&4 breakdown .pdf','Conditional Chains.pdf']; [(print('\\nFILE '+n+'\\n'+ '\\n'.join(p.extract_text() or '' for p in PdfReader(root/n).pages))) for n in names]\"","max_output_tokens":11000}));


## Tool: custom_tool_call_output

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 5.7 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"7e8533","wall_time_seconds":5.4797412,"exit_code":1,"original_token_count":1091,"output":"Traceback (most recent call last):\\r\\n  File \\"<string>\\", line 1, in <module>\\r\\n  File \\"C:\\\\Users\\\\Joshu\\\\AppData\\\\Local\\\\Programs\\\\Python\\\\Python312\\\\Lib\\\\encodings\\\\cp1252.py\\", line 19, in encode\\r\\n    return codecs.charmap_encode(input,self.errors,encoding_table)[0]\\r\\n           ^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^\\r\\nUnicodeEncodeError: \'charmap\' codec can\'t encode character \'\\\\u2192\' in position 1109: character maps to <undefined>\\r\\n\\r\\nFILE Conditional logic intro_.pdf\\r\\nUnderstanding Conditional Logic and\\r\\nContrapositives in LSAT Logical Reasoning\\r\\nClarifying Conditional Logic\\r\\nConditional reasoning, or \\"the art of the if/then,\\" is a fundamental concept on the\\r\\nLSAT that helps you understand logical relationships within arguments.\\r\\nSufficient and Necessary Conditions: A Practical Example\\r\\nImagine this scenario:\\r\\nConditional Statement: \\"If you shop at Macy\'s, then you will get a special discount.\\"\\r\\nWhat do you need to do to get that \\"special discount\\"?\\r\\nIf you think \\"shop at Macy\'s,\\" you\'re right, but that\'s not the full picture. Shopping\\r\\nat Macy\'s is the sufficient condition�it guarantees the discount. However, it might\\r\\nnot be the only way to get the discount. There could be other promotions or coupons.\\r\\nThe statement doesn\'t say shopping at Macy\'s is the only method, just that it is a\\r\\nguaranteed method.\\r\\nWhat we know:\\r\\nShopping at Macy\'s will definitely get you the discount.\\r\\nWhat we don�t know:\\r\\nWhether there are other ways to receive the discount.\\r\\nWhat specifically triggers someone to decide to shop at Macy\'s.\\r\\nGroup 1 and Group 2 Indicators\\r\\nIn conditional logic, indicators help identify whether a statement sets a condition as\\r\\nsufficient or necessary:\\r\\nGroup 1 Indicators (Sufficient): if, when, whenever, all, any, every\\r\\nGroup 2 Indicators (Necessary): only, only if, must, requires\\r\\nThese indicators guide the interpretation of conditional statements, enhancing\\r\\nunderstanding of what actions or outcomes are guaranteed or required.\\r\\nMisconceptions and Clarifications\\r\\nOften, people confuse sufficient for necessary. In our example:\\r\\nSufficient Condition: Shopping at Macy\'s (guarantees the discount)\\r\\nNecessary Condition: Is unknown from the statement. The discount happening\\r\\ndoesn\'t necessarily mean someone shopped at Macy\'s.\\r\\nThe Concept of Contrapositives\\r\\nUnderstanding contrapositives is crucial for interpreting logical statements correctly\\r\\nand efficiently, especially on the LSAT.\\r\\nDefining Contrapositives\\r\\nThe contrapositive of a conditional statement reverses and negates both the conditions\\r\\nbut retains the truth of the original statement.\\r\\nOriginal Conditional: If you shop at Macy\'s (A), then you get a special discount (B).\\r\\nContrapositive: If you do not get a special discount (not B), then you did not shop at\\r\\nMacy\'s (not A).\\r\\nBoth statements are logically equivalent�understanding one helps you confirm the truth\\r\\nof the other.\\r\\nVisual Explanation\\r\\nConsider this visual representation to further clarify the relationship:\\r\\n (This image shows how the original condition and its contrapositive are two sides of\\r\\nthe same coin.)\\r\\nDiagram: Sufficient vs. Necessary\\r\\nThis diagram helps illustrate the relationship between sufficient and necessary\\r\\nconditions:\\r\\nCondition\\r\\nType\\r\\nTrigger\\r\\nCondition\\r\\nResultant\\r\\nGuarantee\\r\\nWhat Cannot Be Inferred\\r\\nSufficient It rains The ground will\\r\\nbe wet\\r\\nGround dryness if it doesn\'t\\r\\nrain\\r\\nNecessary Win the\\r\\nlottery\\r\\nMust buy a ticket Number of tickets bought on\\r\\nwinning\\r\\n (This is a placeholder link, replace with actual visual content.)\\r\\nPop Quiz on Conditional Logic\\r\\nUsing the conditional rule: \\"If you sell your soul to a big law firm, then you will\\r\\nwallow in misery.\\"\\r\\n1. What is a sufficient condition for wallowing in misery?\\r\\nSelling your soul to a big law firm.\\r\\n2. What is a necessary condition for wallowing in misery?\\r\\nWe don�t know. The rule only tells you what is sufficient.\\r\\n3. What is a sufficient condition for selling your soul to a big law firm?\\r\\nWe don�t know. The rule does not provide this information.\\r\\n4. What is a necessary condition for selling your soul to a big law firm?\\r\\nWallowing in misery, according to the rule.\\r\\nConclusion: Leveraging Conditional Logic on the LSAT\\r\\nUnderstanding and effectively applying conditional logic enhances your ability to\\r\\ndissect complex arguments and tackle challenging LSAT questions efficiently.\\r\\n"}'}]

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 0.0 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"958a76","wall_time_seconds":0.0000244,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":2810,"output":"\\r\\nFILE LSAT Logical Reasoning_ Contrapositives and Conditionals( 6 step process).pdf\\r\\nLSAT Logical Reasoning: Contrapositives and\\r\\nConditionals\\r\\nOverview of Conditional Statements\\r\\nGroup 1: Sufficient Condition Indicators\\r\\nKeywords: IF, WHEN, WHERE, ALL, THE ONLY, EVERY, ANY\\r\\nRule: Introduces the sufficient condition.\\r\\nGroup 2: Necessary Condition Indicators\\r\\nKeywords: ONLY IF, ONLY WHEN, ONLY WHERE, ONLY, ALWAYS, MUST, REQUIRES\\r\\nRule: Introduces the necessary condition.\\r\\nSteps to Analyze Conditional Statements\\r\\n1. Identify the logical operator.\\r\\n2. Identify the two main concepts (groups, categories, events, ideas).\\r\\n3. Assign symbols to the two main concepts.\\r\\n4. Apply the translation rule.\\r\\n5. Find the contrapositive.\\r\\n6. Translate back to English.\\r\\nUnderstanding Contrapositives\\r\\nDefinition: Contrapositives are statements that are logically equivalent to the\\r\\noriginal conditional statement.\\r\\nProperties: They are literally the same relationship expressed in reverse form,\\r\\nthe only thing logically equivalent to the original relationship.\\r\\nExample:\\r\\nEnglish: All dogs are unselfish.\\r\\nLawgic: D → ¬S\\r\\nContrapositive in English: If something is selfish, then it’s not a dog.\\r\\nContrapositive in Lawgic: S → ¬D\\r\\nKey Concept: Contrapositives represent the only other way in \\"Lawgic\\" to convey\\r\\na specific conditional relationship.\\r\\nHow to Formulate a Contrapositive(FLIP AND NEGATE )\\r\\n1. Switch the symbols around the arrow.\\r\\n2. Apply negation to each symbol.\\r\\n3. There is no Step 3; it\'s a simple two-step process to ensure clarity.\\r\\nImportance of Contrapositives in LSAT\\r\\nApplication: Essential for solving logical reasoning problems effectively.\\r\\nStrategy: Often provides the correct perspective to answer tricky questions\\r\\ncorrectly.\\r\\nHighlights\\r\\nCritical Insight: \\"Contrapositives will save your life on the LSAT.\\"\\r\\nExample of Logical Equivalence: The contrapositive S → ¬D  is as valid as D →\\r\\n¬S , The original statement and its contrapositive are all you know from your\\r\\nconditional relationship. The LSAT will tempt you to erroneously assume other\\r\\nthings from your conditional. Don\'t fall for these ploys. For example, they\\r\\nwill give you a conditional statement and then say, \\"We know the necessary\\r\\ncondition is happening!\\" When they do this, you should be like, \\"Cool. So I can\\r\\nignore the conditional.\\" The presence of the necessary condition doesn\'t make\\r\\nanything happen.\\r\\nConditional Relationship Activation Chart\\r\\nSituation What has to happen?\\r\\nSufficient\\r\\nPresent\\r\\nI\'m breathing, so there must be air.\\r\\nNecessary\\r\\nAbsent\\r\\nThere\'s no air, so I can\'t breathe.\\r\\nSufficient\\r\\nAbsent\\r\\nNothing! There\'s no breathing happening, so we don\'t know\\r\\nanything else.\\r\\nNecessary\\r\\nPresent\\r\\nNothing! There\'s air around, so we don\'t know anything else.\\r\\nThe last two rows of this chart represent the only two situations that make anything\\r\\nhappen with your conditional relationship. The conditional is just a rule; it takes\\r\\nthe right situation to activate it. Literally anything other than that, two things can\\r\\nhappen and you don\'t need to do anything with your conditional. Without the situations\\r\\nin the first two rows of this chart, the conditional might as well not exist.\\r\\n\\r\\nFILE Group 2 breakdown .pdf\\r\\n1.\\r\\n2.\\r\\n3.\\r\\n4.\\r\\n5.\\r\\n6.\\r\\nGroup 2: Basic Translation - Group 2\\r\\nGROUP 2: ONLY, ONLY IF, ONLY WHEN, ONLY WHERE, ALWAYS, MUST\\r\\nAll the words in this group follow this translation rule:\\r\\nThe ideas introduced by (i.e., immediately following) these words are the \\r\\nnecessary conditions.\\r\\nLet’s Try It:\\r\\nExample Statement:\\r\\n\\"There will be an accomplishment of lasting value only if there is hard work.\\"\\r\\nStep 1: Identify the logical operator.\\r\\nHere, it’s “only if.”\\r\\nStep 2: Identify the two main concepts (or groups, categories, events, or \\r\\nideas).\\r\\nHere, it’s the two events “accomplishment of lasting value” and “there is \\r\\nhard work.”\\r\\nStep 3: Assign symbols to the two main concepts.\\r\\nHere, “ALV” for “accomplishment of lasting value” and “HW” for “there is \\r\\nhard work.”\\r\\nStep 4: Apply the translation rule.\\r\\nThe ideas introduced by these words are the necessary conditions.\\r\\nALV → HW\\r\\nStep 5: Find the contrapositive.\\r\\n/HW → /ALV\\r\\nStep 6: Translate back to English.\\r\\nIf there is no hard work, then there is not an accomplishment of lasting \\r\\nvalue.\\r\\n1\\r\\n•\\r\\n•\\r\\nExplanation:\\r\\nYou see why this is called a “machine”? Because it’s mechanical! You don’t \\r\\nreally have to think very much about these sentences. It’s almost like a \\r\\ncomputer algorithm. You put in the data and it puts out the results. The \\r\\ncomputer doesn’t know what it’s doing. It’s simply following instructions. \\r\\nThat’s what you should do for now. You have to be fast at this, really fast.\\r\\nThere’s an advantage to using a machine. It helps to elucidate the meaning of \\r\\ncertain opaque sentences. A sentence using the “only if” construct, or really \\r\\nany of the constructions in Group 2, probably doesn’t need elucidation no \\r\\nmatter how complicated the ideas may be. But a less clear construct, like \\r\\n“unless” (that’s Group 3), when coupled with more complicated ideas, may \\r\\nspell trouble. But as long as you coldly follow the machine, you’ll be in the \\r\\nclear.\\r\\nLET’S REVIEW:\\r\\nGroup 2 Logical Indicators: only, only if, only when, only where, always, \\r\\nmust, requires.\\r\\nGroup 2 Rule: The idea immediately following the logical indicator is the \\r\\nnecessary condition.\\r\\n2\\r\\n\\r\\nFILE Group 3&4 breakdown .pdf\\r\\n1.\\r\\n2.\\r\\n3.\\r\\n4.\\r\\n5.\\r\\n6.\\r\\nGroup 3: Basic Translation - Group 3\\r\\nGROUP 3: UNLESS, UNTIL, OR, WITHOUT\\r\\nThese are often confusing for students. However, this difficulty can be easily \\r\\novercome by sticking to your translation mechanism.\\r\\nAll the words in this group follow this translation rule:\\r\\nYou pick either idea, then negate that idea, and make that idea the sufficient \\r\\ncondition.\\r\\nLet’s Try It:\\r\\nExample Statement:\\r\\n\\"Don’t go to Hawaii, unless you want to have a good time.\\"\\r\\nStep 1: Identify the logical operator.\\r\\nHere, it’s “unless.”\\r\\nStep 2: Identify the two main concepts (or groups, categories, events, or \\r\\nideas).\\r\\nHere, it’s the two events “not going to Hawaii” and “having a good time.”\\r\\nStep 3: Assign symbols to the two main concepts.\\r\\nHere, “/H” for “not going to Hawaii” and “GT” for “having a good time.”\\r\\nStep 4: Apply the translation rule.\\r\\nPick an idea—“/H”—negate it—“H”—and make it the sufficient condition.\\r\\nH → GT\\r\\nStep 5: Find the contrapositive.\\r\\n/GT → /H\\r\\nStep 6: Translate back to English.\\r\\nIf you don’t want to have a good time, then don’t go to Hawaii.\\r\\n1\\r\\n•\\r\\n•\\r\\nExplanation:\\r\\nThe thing about this group is that it indicates necessity, doesn’t it? \\"Unless\\" is \\r\\nemphasizing the necessity of what comes after it. But then it’s simultaneously \\r\\nwarning you about the consequences of not following through with that \\r\\nnecessity. For instance, with “Don’t go to Hawaii, unless you want to have a \\r\\ngood time,” the “unless” is indicating that “wanting to have a good time” is the \\r\\nnecessary condition. However, the sentence doesn’t explicitly state what it’s \\r\\nnecessary for. It’s implied that it’s the necessary condition for “going to \\r\\nHawaii.”\\r\\nTo avoid confusion, just straightforwardly follow the translation mechanism, \\r\\nand you’ll be golden.\\r\\nLET’S REVIEW:\\r\\nGroup 3 Logical Indicators: unless, until, or, without.\\r\\nGroup 3 Rule: You pick either idea, negate that idea, and make that idea \\r\\nthe sufficient condition.\\r\\nGROUP 4: NO, NONE, NOT BOTH, NEVER, CANNOT\\r\\nAll the words in this group follow this translation rule:\\r\\nYou pick either idea, then negate that idea, and make that idea the necessary \\r\\ncondition.\\r\\nLet’s Try It:\\r\\nExample Statement:\\r\\n2\\r\\n1.\\r\\n2.\\r\\n3.\\r\\n4.\\r\\n5.\\r\\n6.\\r\\n•\\r\\n•\\r\\n\\"None of the Americans attended the dictator’s party.\\"\\r\\nStep 1: Identify the logical operator.\\r\\nHere, it’s “none.”\\r\\nStep 2: Identify the two main concepts (or groups, categories, events, or \\r\\nideas).\\r\\nHere, it’s the two events “being an American” and “attending the dictator’s \\r\\nparty.”\\r\\nStep 3: Assign symbols to the two main concepts.\\r\\nHere, “A” for “being an American” and “ADP” for “attending the dictator’s \\r\\nparty.”\\r\\nStep 4: Apply the translation rule.\\r\\nPick an idea—“A”—negate it—“/A”—and make it the necessary condition.\\r\\nADP → /A\\r\\nStep 5: Find the contrapositive.\\r\\nA → /ADP\\r\\nStep 6: Translate back to English.\\r\\nIf you’re an American, then you did not attend the dictator’s party.\\r\\nExplanation:\\r\\nGroup 4 emphasizes negation. The logical indicators in this group make it clear \\r\\nthat one of the ideas must not occur for the other to be true. For instance, \\r\\n“None of the Americans attended the dictator’s party” tells you that being an \\r\\nAmerican is a necessary condition for not attending. By using negation \\r\\ncorrectly, you can handle these structures without confusion.\\r\\nLET’S REVIEW:\\r\\nGroup 4 Logical Indicators: no, none, not both, never, cannot.\\r\\nGroup 4 Rule: You pick either idea, negate that idea, and make that idea \\r\\nthe necessary condition.\\r\\n3\\r\\n\\r\\nFILE Conditional Chains.pdf\\r\\nConditional Chains\\r\\nConditional statements are like Legos. They are more interesting when combined. You\\r\\ncan latch them together in predictable ways.\\r\\nExample\\r\\nStatement 1:\\r\\nIf I buy an espresso machine, I will end up in the highly-caffeinated poor house.\\r\\nStatement 2:\\r\\nIf I end up in the highly-caffeinated poor house, I will finally be cool enough to\\r\\nwear non-ironic plaid.\\r\\nLet’s think each of these statements through. Let’s place ourselves in this situation\\r\\nand see what we know.\\r\\nSay I go ahead and make the poor decision to buy an espresso machine. What do we know\\r\\nabout me?\\r\\nI’m in the poor house.\\r\\nNow what do we know from that? We know enough to activate the second\\r\\nconditional! Since I’m in the poor house, I can finally wear plaid (still not\\r\\nthere in real life).\\r\\nNotice, we only had enough information to activate the first conditional at the\\r\\nbeginning, but we ended up at the end of the second conditional. What witchcraft is\\r\\nthis? It works because these statements were set up to chain, meaning they naturally\\r\\nconnect.\\r\\nHow to Know When You Can Chain\\r\\nThese two statements chained because the necessary condition of the first statement\\r\\nmatched the sufficient condition of the second statement. That’s a pretty wordy way of\\r\\nexplaining something that is simple when you examine it visually. You’ll know you can\\r\\nchain when you can perfectly overlap two conditions and nothing conflicts.\\r\\nLook at the following two conditionals:\\r\\nL → M M → N\\r\\nI want you to imagine picking up that second conditional and laying it on top of the\\r\\nfirst one. You can latch in the second conditional at the M  on the first one. It\\r\\nfits there. That’s your interlocking Lego point. There’s room for the N  to come\\r\\nafter the M  in the first conditional; nothing’s there to block it. So you end up\\r\\nwith:\\r\\nL → M → N\\r\\nAwesome!\\r\\nThis is how I personally chain conditionals. No jargon about similar\\r\\nsufficient/necessary. That’s not intuitive. What is intuitive is looking to see if you\\r\\nhave the same variable and an available empty space.\\r\\n"}'}]

Test 41 Section 3 Question 24 Newscaster In order for the.\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n## Source: Copy of Conditional Questions (Timed).gdoc\\r\\n\\r\\n**Path:** Copy of Conditional Questions (Timed).gdoc  **Modified:** 2025-07-17T21:07:35.444000+00:00  **Words:** 383  **Doc ID:** 1h0l1zzQ0GYQG-gW08v9d0qnA6iYt_pcJKU_vUNDe_D0\\r\\n\\r\\nConditional. Focus on finding Conditional Indicator words and using them as a way to organize how you write or think about the If-Then rule. Write or think through the contrapositive as well. Beware illegal Reversals or Negations. Conditional Logic by type Recap: Principle (the left side of the principle matches the evidence, the right side matches the conclusion). Parallel (diagramming these arguments with algebraic recipes allows us to be more picky and decisive when evaluating answers). Assumption (the conclusion gives us an A to Z logic path we want to prove, and the evidence + the correct answer choice assemble together to create that A to Z path). Must Be True / Most Supported (multiple conditionals often chain together, and provided facts often match the contrapositive-trigger of a conditional and allow us to infer the contrapositive-outcome). Flaw / Parallel Flaw (flaw + conditional logic usually means the necessary vs sufficient flaw using a rule in an illegal backwards or opposite fashion). instructions: Try these 10 questions in 22.5 minutes. Try to answer them all within your time limit, then circle back and confirm your thinking (untimed) before confirming your answers and reading the explanations, if available. CONDITIONAL KEYWORDS: Put on Left (sufficient conditions): if, when, any, each, all, every, the only, whenever, whoever. Put on Right (necessary conditions): only if, must, requires, ensures, always, implies, presupposes, depends on, guarantees. If-Not (negated version on left): unless, without, until, except. Negative to Positive conversions: No A are B = All A are not B. Not all A are B = Some A are not B. Few A are B = Most A are not B. Problems to Complete (10 total): PrepTest 130 Section 3 Question 13. PrepTest 130 Section 3 Question 14. PrepTest 131 Section 1 Question 16. PrepTest 131 Section 1 Question 19. PrepTest 134 Section 2 Question 3. PrepTest 134 Section 2 Question 22. PrepTest 134 Section 2 Question 23. PrepTest 135 Section 2 Question 15. PrepTest 125 Section 4 Question 23. PrepTest 125 Section 4 Question 25. Pause! For every mistake you made in this set, complete the sections below in as much detail as possible: Why did I eliminate the right answer? Why did I choose this wrong answer? What can I do next time to get this right? Additional notes.\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n## Source: intuition technique when stuck( conditional logic) .pdf\\r\\n\\r\\n**Path:** LSAT DUMP/LR/Conditional/Notes/intuition technique when stuck( conditional logic) .pdf  **Modified:** 2024-05-16T01:17:45+00:00  **Words:** 370  **Doc ID:** None\\r\\n\\r\\nHow to Figure Out Which Is Which: The What Test vs. Personalized If/Then The What Test The What Test is designed to resolve ambiguities in sentences with conditional indicators by pinpointing exactly what the indicator refers to. How to Perform the What Test This test involves adding a \\"what\\" to whichever side of the indicator makes grammatical sense, helping clarify the target of the condition. If what? People who what? Depends on what? When what? Each what? Need what? Any what? Must what? Have\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n## Source: intuition technique when stuck( conditional logic) .gdoc\\r\\n\\r\\n**Path:** LSAT DUMP/LR/Conditional/Notes/intuition technique when stuck( conditional logic) .gdoc  **Modified:** 2025-06-23T03:31:37.970000+00:00  **Words:** 370  **Doc ID:** 1ZthkbR3x5oHk9Rqv2ZOzR6j_tZ4HDsM45S_K0hBZlpA\\r\\n\\r\\nHow to Figure Out Which Is Which The What Test vs. Personalized If/Then. The What Test. The What Test is designed to resolve ambiguities in sentences with conditional indicators by pinpointing exactly what the indicator refers to. How to Perform the What Test. This test involves adding a what to whichever side of the indicator makes grammatical sense helping clarify the target of the condition. If what? People who what? Depends on what? When what? Each what? Need what? Any what? Must what? Have to what? All what? Whats necessary? Whats essential? Every what? Whats required? Whats the precondition? In order to what? Only (if) what? This test is typically unnecessary for straightforward indicators like if and all but proves invaluable for more confusing indicators such as depends and required. Example of The What Test. Sentence: Air is required for breathing. Implementation: Question: What is required in this sentence? Answer: Air is the thing that is required. Personalized If/Then Strategy. Personalized If/Then is a technique used to translate conditional statements into personal contexts simplifying understanding and ensuring correct logical diagramming. How to Create a Personalized If/Then. 1. Understand the casual non-conditional meaning of the sentence. 2. Take a guess at which half of the statement is the sufficient condition. 3. Put yourself in the situation of your guess and ask what you know about yourself. 4. If your guess is correct you will find that the necessary condition has to pertain. Example Translation into Personalized If/Then. Original Statement: Orchids depend on frequent conversation. Casual Meaning: Orchids need frequent conversation. Guess: Orchids as the sufficient condition. Personalization: If Im an orchid what do I know about me? I depend on frequent conversation. If Im an orchid then I have frequent conversation. This method not only aids in understanding the conditional but also verifies its correctness. Incorrect guesses can be easily identified and corrected through this personal context. Advantages of Personalized If/Then. Helps intuitively understand conditionals by imagining yourself as part of the scenario. Provides confidence throughout tests especially in correctly interpreting conditionals not phrased as if/then statements. Both the What Test and Personalized If/Then Strategy are crucial tools in deciphering and applying conditional logic particularly in competitive exams like the LSAT.\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n## Source: Conditional Chains.pdf\\r\\n\\r\\n**Path:** LSAT DUMP/LR/Conditional/Notes/Conditional Chains.pdf  **Modified:** 2024-07-02T20:46:11+00:00  **Words:** 352  **Doc ID:** None\\r\\n\\r\\nConditional Chains Conditional statements are like Legos. They are more interesting when combined. You can latch them together in predictable ways. Example Statement 1: If I buy an espresso machine, I will end up in the highly-caffeinated poor house. Statement 2: If I end up in the highly-caffeinated poor house, I will finally be cool enough to wear non-ironic plaid. Let’s think each of these statements through. Let’s place ourselves in this situation and see what we know. Say I go ahead and mak\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n## Source: conditional worksheet.gdoc\\r\\n\\r\\n**Path:** LSAT DUMP/LR/Conditional/conditional worksheet.gdoc  **Modified:** 2025-08-13T03:04:32.059000+00:00  **Words:** 251  **Doc ID:** 1w4y1O0hunEBLCqr121xBLWoNUy5URqXhHHkvWZO8X8U\\r\\n\\r\\nConditional Rephrasing Worksheet. Instructions and Example. Ill give you a conditional statement. You will rephrase it into the simplest conditional phrasing if/then format. Example Statement: You cant master the LSAT without mastering conditional reasoning. Credited Answers: If you master the LSAT then you master conditional reasoning. If you dont master conditional reasoning you wont master the LSAT. Explanation: If you cant master X without Y Y is required in order to master X. That can be rephrased into if/then format in two ways: 1. If you master X then you have Y. 2. If you dont have Y you cant master X. Tasks: 1. Consider the conditional relationship at play in each statement. 2. Rephrase the statement using if/then format. Statements and Answers. 1. Sentence A policy change will occur only if the board approves it. Translation: Contrapositive: 2. Sentence Keeping plants healthy requires regular watering and sunlight. 3. Sentence No solution will be effective unless it addresses the root problem. 4. Sentence Whenever a fire alarm sounds the building must be evacuated immediately. 5. Sentence If the weather remains cold the lake will \\r\\n"}'}]

## assistant

Reviewed all seven local Conditional Logic pages, plus five recovered original PDFs in full. Many megadoc PDF/DOCX entries are truncated previews; the GDOC entries include fuller text. Do not claim all underlying originals were reviewed.

Joshua’s actual methods missing from the initial content:

- **The What Test:** “What is the indicator referring to?” Targets can precede or follow indicators. “Air is required for breathing” → “What is required?” → air.
- **Personalized If/Then:** understand ordinary meaning; guess the sufficient half; put yourself in that situation; ask what must be true. Full four-step evidence is in `intuition technique when stuck( conditional logic) .gdoc`, not the truncated ending of `Conditional Understanding.gdoc`.
- **Six-step translation:** identify logical operator → identify two concepts → assign symbols → apply translation rule → find contrapositive → translate back to English.
- **Recall and Rephrase:** named training in homework notes, alongside memorizing Groups 1–4 and six steps.
- **Visual chaining:** full `Conditional Chains.pdf` uses interlocking Lego overlap: same variable plus an available space to extend the chain.
- **Chaining versus triggering:** explicitly different outputs, new rules versus new facts.
- **Mistake review:** “Why did I eliminate the right answer?”, “Why did I choose this wrong answer?”, “What can I do next time to get this right?”

A defensible instructional sequence synthesized from these notes is meaning and sufficient/necessary relationships → indicator targets and Groups 1–2 → six-step translation/contrapositive/rephrasing → Groups 3–4 and mixed translation → chaining and triggering → question-type application → quantifiers afterward, as Joshua requested. The notes do not contain one explicit canonical ordered syllabus.

Current UI logistics must be separated from student teaching. “Your assigned stage” and “Build the reasoning from your assigned stage” describe assignment mechanics, not conditional instruction. Joshua assigns work separately; the curriculum should teach interpreting, translating, contraposing, linking, and applying conditional claims.

Source errors should remain explicit and should not be propagated:

- Older Group 2 notes assume the target immediately follows the indicator; newer What Test notes expressly reject this shortcut.
- Intro PDF calls the necessary condition unknown in Macy’s → discount, although discount is necessary for Macy’s under that rule.
- Six-step PDF’s activation-chart closing prose mistakenly names the last two rows; its chart correctly activates sufficient-present and necessary-absent.
- Group 4 explanatory prose incorrectly says American is necessary for not attending; its diagrams are correct.
- Older worksheet’s 5/5 advancement gate is a historical worksheet instruction, not authorization for a global mastery lock.

Read-only, no files changed. Findings and exact source anchors sent to the content worker.

## assistant

No substantive issues found. The additions accurately reflect Joshua’s What Test, Personalized If/Then, six-step translation process, rephrasing practice, and visual chaining method.

The new wording correctly checks meaning rather than treating indicator position or a guessed direction as proof. It also avoids propagating the original notes’ identified logical errors.

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 3.0 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"f001e3","wall_time_seconds":2.7340446,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":8894,"output":"Warning: truncated output (original token count: 8894)\\nTotal output lines: 295\\n\\nstudents/sofia/assignment-log.md:13:# Sofia Assignment Log\\nstudents/sofia/assignment-log.md:17:## Assignments\\nstudents/sofia/assignment-log.md:22:## Interactions\\nstudents/sofia/assignment-log.md:26:## 2026-09-11\\nstudents/sofia/assignment-log.md:32:## 2026-09-25\\nstudents/sofia/recaps/2026-09-11-recap.md:9:posted: false\\nstudents/sofia/recaps/2026-09-11-recap.md:13:# Conditional Must Be True: Facts, Rules, and Bridges\\nstudents/sofia/recaps/2026-09-11-recap.md:15:A conditional Must Be True question has two basic moves. You either connect one rule to another, or use a fact to activate a rule. Before you combine anything, identify which kind of move the stimulus gives you.\\nstudents/sofia/recaps/2026-09-11-recap.md:19:## The method at a glance\\nstudents/sofia/recaps/2026-09-11-recap.md:23:| Rule + rule | **Chaining** | A new rule |\\nstudents/sofia/recaps/2026-09-11-recap.md:24:| Fact + rule | **Triggering** | A new fact |\\nstudents/sofia/recaps/2026-09-11-recap.md:28:## 1. Know what each statement does\\nstudents/sofia/recaps/2026-09-11-recap.md:37:### Look for certainty, not just a keyword\\nstudents/sofia/recaps/2026-09-11-recap.md:41:The real test is simple: if the first part is true, **must** the second part follow? If not, you do not have a conditional rule.\\nstudents/sofia/recaps/2026-09-11-recap.md:43:## 2. Chaining: rule + rule produces a new rule\\nstudents/sofia/recaps/2026-09-11-recap.md:45:When the stimulus gives you several rules and no fact that activates them, look for a **bridge**.\\nstudents/sofia/recaps/2026-09-11-recap.md:47:A bridge is the overlapping idea that sits at the end of one rule and the beginning of another. It is the middle step that lets you move from the first idea to the last.\\nstudents/sofia/recaps/2026-09-11-recap.md:49:A usable bridge appears in one of two ways:\\nstudents/sofia/recaps/2026-09-11-recap.md:54:### Step 1: Diagram each rule\\nstudents/sofia/recaps/2026-09-11-recap.md:61:### Step 2: Find the overlapping idea\\nstudents/sofia/recaps/2026-09-11-recap.md:63:`trained` is the bridge because it is:\\nstudents/sofia/recaps/2026-09-11-recap.md:70:### Step 3: Connect the loose ends\\nstudents/sofia/recaps/2026-09-11-recap.md:76:The bridge is used to build the inference, but it usually disappears from the new rule. The conclusion connects the two ideas left on the outside: `pilot` and `licensed`.\\nstudents/sofia/recaps/2026-09-11-recap.md:78:### A repeated word is not always a bridge\\nstudents/sofia/recaps/2026-09-11-recap.md:98:### Sometimes the bridge is hidden in a contrapositive\\nstudents/sofia/recaps/2026-09-11-recap.md:113:Contraposing can reveal a bridge. Reversing a rule cannot.\\nstudents/sofia/recaps/2026-09-11-recap.md:115:### The answer may test any link you had to build\\nstudents/sofia/recaps/2026-09-11-recap.md:123:then `A → C`, `B → D`, and `A → D` are all valid inferences. The test can reward any connection that required you to move through at least one bridge.\\nstudents/sofia/recaps/2026-09-11-recap.md:125:## 3. Triggering: fact + rule produces a new fact\\nstudents/sofia/recaps/2026-09-11-recap.md:129:### The fact confirms the trigger\\nstudents/sofia/recaps/2026-09-11-recap.md:140:### The fact denies the required result\\nstudents/sofia/recaps/2026-09-11-recap.md:156:### The two facts that do not activate the rule\\nstudents/sofia/recaps/2026-09-11-recap.md:174:## 4. Know the four possible moves\\nstudents/sofia/recaps/2026-09-11-recap.md:185:### Use the contrapositive when the move is hidden\\nstudents/sofia/recaps/2026-09-11-recap.md:189:### Diagram when the notation will do work\\nstudents/sofia/recaps/2026-09-11-recap.md:200:### Test a conditional answer by its trigger\\nstudents/sofia/recaps/2026-09-11-recap.md:202:When an answer choice gives you a new conditional rule, start with its trigger. Ask:\\nstudents/sofia/recaps/2026-09-11-recap.md:210:## 5. Use the same process every time\\nstudents/sofia/recaps/2026-09-11-recap.md:216:5. Check the answer choices for that inference. For a conditional answer, test its trigger first. If an answer reverses or negates the rule, eliminate it.\\nstudents/sofia/recaps/2026-09-11-recap.md:218:## Practice\\nstudents/sofia/recaps/2026-09-11-recap.md:220:- [ ] Complete the assigned conditional-diagramming practice. Label the trigger and result, then write the contrapositive until that move becomes automatic.\\nstudents/sofia/recaps/2026-09-11-recap.md:227:## Notes for me (not posted)\\nstudents/sofia/recaps/2026-09-11-recap.md:229:- **Weak spots:** sufficient versus necessary placement; second-guessing a correct reading after diagramming; separating situational facts from conditional rules.\\nstudents/sofia/recaps/2026-09-11-recap.md:230:- **Follow-ups on my side:** add four session credits or otherwise reconcile the two or three missed sessions; assign high-volume conditional practice; clean up the Read, Recall, Colour instructions/report card.\\nstudents/yahunatan-zetvy/recaps/2026-09-16-recap.md:9:posted: false\\r\\nstudents/yahunatan-zetvy/recaps/2026-09-16-recap.md:15:**Current student-facing lesson:** [Facts, Rules, and What Follows](../../../brainstorms/2026-09-30-yahunatan-post-session-recap/conditional-reasoning-v10.html). The earlier versions below are preserved history. The marked V10 block is the current export source.\\r\\nstudents/yahunatan-zetvy/recaps/2026-09-16-recap.md:17:# Conditional Must Be True: Keep the Whole Statement\\r\\nstudents/yahunatan-zetvy/recaps/2026-09-16-recap.md:19:Only people willing to compromise should undergo mediation. This tells you what is required for mediation to be appropriate. It does not tell you who actually attends, or whether willingness alone is enough. Keeping those distinctions intact is the work of conditional reasoning.\\r\\nstudents/yahunatan-zetvy/recaps/2026-09-16-recap.md:21:## Combine what the statements guarantee\\r\\nstudents/yahunatan-zetvy/recaps/2026-09-16-recap.md:33:### Check your reasoning\\r\\nstudents/yahunatan-zetvy/recaps/2026-09-16-recap.md:39:## Put the requirement on the right\\r\\nstudents/yahunatan-zetvy/recaps/2026-09-16-recap.md:67:### Check your reasoning\\r\\nstudents/yahunatan-zetvy/recaps/2026-09-16-recap.md:73:## Keep should in the statement\\r\\nstudents/yahunatan-zetvy/recaps/2026-09-16-recap.md:83:### Check your reasoning\\r\\nstudents/yahunatan-zetvy/recaps/2026-09-16-recap.md:89:## Reverse and negate to get the contrapositive\\r\\nstudents/yahunatan-zetvy/recaps/2026-09-16-recap.md:107:### Test every possible combination\\r\\nstudents/yahunatan-zetvy/recaps/2026-09-16-recap.md:118:### Check your reasoning\\r\\nstudents/yahunatan-zetvy/recaps/2026-09-16-recap.md:124:## A statement about a whole group can be a rule\\r\\nstudents/yahunatan-zetvy/recaps/2026-09-16-recap.md:134:This is a conditional relationship even without the word if. If a person belongs to the named group, the description applies to that person. It does not establish that any particular person belongs to that group.\\r\\nstudents/yahunatan-zetvy/recaps/2026-09-16-recap.md:146:### Check your reasoning\\r\\nstudents/yahunatan-zetvy/recaps/2026-09-16-recap.md:152:## Do not invent a choice between two activities\\r\\nstudents/yahunatan-zetvy/recaps/2026-09-16-recap.md:162:### Check your reasoning\\r\\nstudents/yahunatan-zetvy/recaps/2026-09-16-recap.md:168:## Use the same process on a new question\\r\\nstudents/yahunatan-zetvy/recaps/2026-09-16-recap.md:179:### Put the moves together\\r\\nstudents/yahunatan-zetvy/recaps/2026-09-16-recap.md:197:## Before the next session\\r\\nstudents/yahunatan-zetvy/recaps/2026-09-16-recap.md:199:Complete the conditional-diagramming drills first, then move into the LSAT questions. The focus is translating and diagramming conditionals until the move becomes automatic.\\r\\nstudents/yahunatan-zetvy/recaps/2026-09-16-recap.md:201:## 2026-09-30 concept extension: Choose the move the information supports\\r\\nstudents/yahunatan-zetvy/recaps/2026-09-16-recap.md:205:## Apply a fact to a rule\\r\\nstudents/yahunatan-zetvy/recaps/2026-09-16-recap.md:219:### Check your reasoning\\r\\nstudents/yahunatan-zetvy/recaps/2026-09-16-recap.md:225:## Find the two starting points a rule gives you\\r\\nstudents/yahunatan-zetvy/recaps/2026-09-16-recap.md:250:### Check your reasoning\\r\\nstudents/yahunatan-zetvy/recaps/2026-09-16-recap.md:256:## Link rules before applying a fact\\r\\nstudents/yahunatan-zetvy/recaps/2026-09-16-recap.md:268:### Check your reasoning\\r\\nstudents/yahunatan-zetvy/recaps/2026-09-16-recap.md:274:## Shared words do not always create a chain\\r\\nstudents/yahunatan-zetvy/recaps/2026-09-16-recap.md:288:### Check your reasoning\\r\\nstudents/yahunatan-zetvy/recaps/2026-09-16-recap.md:294:## Negate the whole condition\\r\\nstudents/yahunatan-zetvy/recaps/2026-09-16-recap.md:314:### Check your reasoning\\r\\nstudents/yahunatan-zetvy/recaps/2026-09-16-recap.md:320:## Choose your next move\\r\\nstudents/yahunatan-zetvy/recaps/2026-09-16-recap.md:329:### Put the moves together\\r\\nstudents/yahunatan-zetvy/recaps/2026-09-16-recap.md:343:## 2026-09-30 concept-first revision\\r\\nstudents/yahunatan-zetvy/recaps/2026-09-16-recap.md:346:# Facts, Rules, and What Follows\\r\\nstudents/yahunatan-zetvy/recaps/2026-09-16-recap.md:356:## 01 | Start with the fact you actually have\\r\\nstudents/yahunatan-zetvy/recaps/2026-09-16-recap.md:366:The rule alone does not tell us that any applications were submitted late. We needed the fact to establish that we had a case to which the rule applies.
