[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 4.7 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"efaf84","wall_time_seconds":4.1222925,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":6727,"output":"{\\"id\\":\\"089ce68a-4eb4-4acc-90a1-cd4d5ac4f30a\\",\\"title\\":\\"Conditional Logic: The Breakdown\\",\\"status\\":\\"assigned\\",\\"visible_to_student\\":true,\\"characters\\":56174,\\"body\\":\\" The Breakdown Eleven parts, one at a time. The cheat sheet tells you what to write. This tells you why it is right. The arrow is certainty. If the left side is true, the right side has to be true. Everything below is a consequence of that one sentence. 01 Words that put it on the left 19 words 02 Words that put it on the right 16 words 03 Words that negate the left 4 words 04 Words that negate the right 5 words 05 Words that are on no list the cold ones 06 And, or, and the flip the three steps 07 Both directions at once biconditionals 08 Not conditionals at all few, not all 09 The two moves that are never allowed the illegal ones 10 The two things you do with a rule chain, trigger 11 When to draw at all setup, not answer 01 Words that put it on the left These are rules, universals and guarantees. All three say the same thing: when this, then that. Whatever the word attaches to is the trigger. It goes on the left, exactly as written, and nothing gets negated. It shows up like this If A, then B. B whenever A. Every A is B. All A are B. Ask yourself \\\\\\"If what?\\\\\\" or \\\\\\"every who or what?\\\\\\" Whatever answers it goes on the left. Which gives you If left, right. right if left. If it rains, practice is cancelled. rain &rarr; practice cancelled All 19 words, each in a sentence if If it rains, practice is cancelled. rain &rarr; practice cancelled when When the alarm sounds, the doors lock. alarm sounds &rarr; doors lock whenever Whenever she drills, she reviews it after. drills &rarr; reviews after wherever Wherever the trail floods, it closes. trail floods &rarr; trail closes anytime Anytime the server restarts, you sign in again. server restarts &rarr; sign in again provided that You may retake it provided that you wait 30 days. waited 30 days &rarr; may retake in the event that In the event that the flight is cancelled, you get a refund. flight cancelled &rarr; refund all All members pay dues. member &rarr; pays dues any Any late paper loses points. late paper &rarr; loses points every Every witness was interviewed. witness &rarr; interviewed each Each application needs a signature. application &rarr; needs signature everyone Everyone who enters signs in. enters &rarr; signs in everything Everything on the shelf is discounted. on the shelf &rarr; discounted leads to A tie in the vote leads to a runoff. tie &rarr; runoff produces Heating the rail produces expansion. heat the rail &rarr; expansion results in Missing the deadline results in disqualification. miss deadline &rarr; disqualified guarantees A winning ticket guarantees the prize. winning ticket &rarr; prize ensures A signed receipt ensures the delivery was logged. signed receipt &rarr; delivery logged always A missed payment always adds a late fee. missed payment &rarr; late fee Diagram this. If you score a 160, you get the scholarship. Check it 160 &rarr; scholarship The idea the word attaches to goes left, word for word. No rearranging, no negating. 02 Words that put it on the right These are requirements and implications. The word names what has to be there, so that idea goes on the right, exactly as written. Same sentence as the one above, one word different, and the arrow points the other way. It shows up like this A only if B. Only A are B. A requires B. B is necessary for A. Ask yourself \\\\\\"What is this word naming as required?\\\\\\" Whatever answers it goes on the right. Which gives you left only if right. right is required for left. You pass only if you attend. pass &rarr; attend All 16 words, each in a sentence only Only members may vote. vote &rarr; member only if You pass only if you attend. pass &rarr; attend only when The gate opens only when the light is green. gate opens &rarr; light green requires Admission requires a transcript. admission &rarr; transcript required A permit is required to build. build &rarr; permit prerequisite Calculus is a prerequisite for physics. physics &rarr; calculus must Applicants must submit two essays. applicant &rarr; two essays needs The plant needs light to bloom. blooms &rarr; light essential Oxygen is essential to combustion. combustion &rarr; oxygen necessary A quorum is necessary for a vote. vote &rarr; quorum depends on The picnic depends on good weather. picnic &rarr; good weather then If the bill passes, then it becomes law. bill passes &rarr; becomes law implies An even number implies a factor of 2. even &rarr; factor of 2 signals A siren signals an emergency. siren &rarr; emergency means A red tag means it is sold. red tag &rarr; sold will If the deadline passes, the form will lock. deadline passes &rarr; form locks Diagram this. You get the scholarship only if you score a 160. Check it scholarship &rarr; 160 One word separates this from the sentence above, and the arrow reverses. Slow down every time you see only and ask what it is marking as required. 03 Words that negate the left All four are \\\\\\"if not\\\\\\" in disguise. The attached idea is what is required, so it goes right, and the other side picks up a NOT. If you would rather not negate anything, swap the word for \\\\\\"if not\\\\\\" and put that on the left instead. Both routes give the same rule, one as the flip of the other. It shows up like this A unless B. No A without B. Not A until B. Ask yourself Read the word as \\\\\\"if not\\\\\\", then put that on the left and negate it. Which gives you left unless right. Or: if not right, then not left. You cannot enter unless you have a pass. enter &rarr; pass All four, each in a sentence unless You cannot enter unless you have a pass. enter &rarr; pass until You cannot leave until the bell rings. leave &rarr; bell rang without No one enters without a badge. enter &rarr; badge except Everyone may enter except minors. enter &rarr; NOT a minor Diagram this. You cannot submit the form without a signature. Check it submit &rarr; signature Which is the same rule as NOT signature &rarr; NOT submit. The two routes always give you a rule and its flip, so if yours came out as the other one, you are still right. 04 Words that negate the right The attached idea is the trigger and goes left, but the negative belongs to the second half of the sentence. Rewrite it as a positive before you draw and it stops being confusing: \\\\\\"no A are B\\\\\\" is \\\\\\"all A are not B.\\\\\\" It shows up like this No A is B. None of the A are B. A is never B. A cannot be B. Ask yourself Rewrite it as a positive first, then draw what you rewrote. Which gives you No left is right. Which is left &rarr; NOT right. No freshman may apply. freshman &rarr; NOT apply All five, each in a sentence no No freshman may apply. freshman &rarr; NOT apply none None of the drafts were saved. draft &rarr; NOT saved never A draft is never final. draft &rarr; NOT final cannot A member cannot vote twice. member &rarr; NOT vote twice not Paper applications are not accepted. paper application &rarr; NOT accepted Diagram this. No expired badge opens the door. Check it expired badge &rarr; NOT opens door The NOT sits on the right. Putting it on the left is the most common way this group goes wrong. 05 Words that are on no list Some words have a home in the lists above. Others you will meet cold. Either way the same question settles it: what is guaranteed, and what is required. Whatever sets the rule off goes left. Whatever has to be there goes right. That works whether the word made a list or not. It shows up like this prerequisite &rarr; right depends on &rarr; right will &rarr; right the moment that &rarr; left Ask yourself What is guaranteed here, and what is required? Which gives you The requirement goes right, even when it happens first. Spotting the gap is a prerequisite to picking the assumption. pick assumption &rarr; spot gap Diagram this. Learning to walk is a prerequisite to running. Check it running &rarr; learned to walk Learning to walk is the requirement, so it goes on the right, even though it happens first. 06 And, or, and the flip A rule and its flip are two sides of the same coin. The flip says: when the right side is not true, the left side cannot be true either. Flipping a rule is what the test is really checking. Three steps, and the third is where the points are. A and B &rarr; C NOT C &rarr; NOT A or NOT B 01 Swap What was on the right goes on the left. 02 Negate Positive becomes negative, negative becomes positive. 03 Switch the connector Every and becomes an or, every or becomes an and. If C did not happen, at least one of A or B is missing, and you cannot say which. Writing and there claims both are missing, which is more than the rule ever gave you. Going the other way, A &rarr; B or C becomes NOT B and NOT C &rarr; NOT A . 07 Both directions at once Some sentences hand you the rule going both ways. Two words do it, and they do not work the same way. if and only if The if puts it left and the only if puts it right, so it is telling you to do both. Also then and only then, when and only when. otherwise It hands you a second rule covering the other case. Write both down, flip both, and the arrow runs both ways. Either both happen or neither does. Why otherwise runs both ways Jeff takes a vacation if he wins the lottery. Otherwise, he does not. That is two rules, not one. lottery &rarr; vacation NOT lottery &rarr; NOT vacation Flip both and they collapse into a double arrow. The trap: one rule plus its own flip is not this. The deposit clears, otherwise the seat is released gives you only NOT deposit &rarr; released . Flipping that is the same rule said backwards, not a second direction. The sentence has to state both cases. 08 Not conditionals at all These sit next to no and behave nothing like it. They count. They do not guarantee, so do not draw an arrow. Few A are B is most A are not B Not all A are B is some A are not B Not all the essays were graded tells you at least one was not graded. An arrow would say every essay behaves the same way, which is more than the sentence gave you. Flip these to the positive and leave them as statements. 09 The two moves that are never allowed Given A &rarr; B , four things can happen and only two of them prove anything. When the trigger is missing the rule simply disappears. It has no power over you, so it tells you nothing. A is true B has to be true. B is false A cannot be true. A is false Nothing follows. Saying B is false is the illegal move. B is true Nothing follows. Saying A is true is reading the arrow backwards. Try it. The rule is if the alarm sounds, the doors lock. You are told the doors are locked. What follows? Check it Nothing follows. The doors could be locked for any number of reasons. You were handed the right side and asked to claim the left. When a flaw answer says the author confuses a necessary condition with a sufficient one, this is what it is describing. 10 The two things you do with a rule Everything above gets the arrow onto the page. From there the test only ever asks for two things. Chain it to another rule and you get a new rule. Trigger it with a fact and you get a new fact. Chaining Whenever you have more than one conditional, ask one question: do these connect? If the right side of one rule is the left side of another, they link, and the link is the inference. A &rarr; B &rarr; C &rarr; D flips whole NOT D &rarr; NOT C &rarr; NOT B &rarr; NOT A Start at the far end and walk it backwards. The test usually wants the front joined to the back, so A &rarr; D is the headline answer. But every shorter run is an inference too: A &rarr; C , B &rarr; D , NOT C &rarr; NOT A . Triggering A rule sits there doing nothing until a fact sets it off. Hand it the left side and the right side has to follow. Hand it a missing piece of the right side and the rest has to carry the load. rule A &rarr; B or C fact A fact NOT C so B Which one is being asked for? Look at the shape of the answers. If they read if this, then that , you are chaining and the answer is a rule. If they read as a flat statement, you are triggering and the answer is a fact. 11 When to draw at all Diagramming is setup, not the answer. Draw only when there is a reason to. DRAW Two or more conditionals that could link. Chain them and look for what connects the two ends. DRAW The conclusion is itself a conditional. The author is claiming one thing guarantees another. DO NOT One conditional in an ordinary argument. Hear it as a requirement and keep reading. The missing link: an argument gives you A &rarr; B and concludes A &rarr; C . There is a hole in the chain, and the piece that fills it is B &rarr; C , or its flip, and that piece is the answer. Test yourself Say the answer out loud before you open each one, and you will know which part still needs work. unless Negate left. The attached idea goes right, the other side takes a NOT. Or read it as if not and put that on the left. only if Right. Whatever follows it goes on the right, exactly as written. The three steps of the flip Swap the sides, negate both, and switch every and to an or. You are given the right side of a rule. What follows? Nothing. Claiming the left from the right is reading the arrow backwards, and it is the most commonly tested flaw on the test. few A are B Not a conditional. It means most A are not B. It counts, it does not guarantee, so no arrow. A &rarr; B, B &rarr; C, C &rarr; D. What is the flip of the whole chain? NOT D &rarr; NOT C &rarr; NOT B &rarr; NOT A. Start at the far end and walk it backwards. The headline inference is A &rarr; D, but every shorter run counts too. Rule plus rule, and rule plus fact. What does each give you? Rule plus rule chains into a new rule. Rule plus fact triggers a new fact. The shape of the answer choices tells you which one is being asked for. When is it worth drawing? When two or more rules could link, or when the conclusion is itself a conditional. A single conditional in an ordinary argument is something you hear, not something you write down. \\"}\\n{\\"id\\":\\"75133805-ff98-4dea-8851-168205733da4\\",\\"title\\":\\"Conditional Logic: Cheat Sheet\\",\\"status\\":\\"assigned\\",\\"visible_to_student\\":true,\\"characters\\":35460,\\"body\\":\\" Conditional Logic Every conditional is one sentence with a trigger and a requirement. One word tells you which is which. trigger what sets the rule off requirement what has to be there Read every rule as the left requires the right, and the ones that link get easy to spot. There are only four moves Every word on this page does one of them. Find the word, do what it says. 01 Left A &rarr; B The word\'s idea is the trigger. It goes on the left, exactly as written. 02 Right B &rarr; A The word\'s idea is what is required. It goes on the right, exactly as written. 03 Negate left B &rarr; A, and a NOT The word\'s idea goes right, and the other side picks up a NOT. 04 Negate right A &rarr; NOT B The word\'s idea goes left, and the second half flips. The word names what has to be there, so that idea goes on the right, exactly as written. Ask \\\\\\"what is this word naming as required?\\\\\\" Same sentence as the one above, one word different, and the arrow points the other way. Rewrite it as a positive before you draw and it stops being confusing: \\\\\\"no A are B\\\\\\" is \\\\\\"all A are not B.\\\\\\" No freshman may apply. freshman &rarr; NOT apply All five, each in a sentence no No freshman may apply. freshman &rarr; NOT apply none None of the drafts were saved. draft &rarr; NOT saved never A draft is never final. draft &rarr; NOT final cannot A member cannot vote twice. member &rarr; NOT vote twice not Paper applications are not accepted. paper application &rarr; NOT accepted NO LIST the ones you meet cold Words that are on no list Some words have a home in the lists above. Others you will meet cold. Either way the same question settles it: what is guaranteed, and what is required. Whatever sets the rule off goes left. Whatever has to be there goes right. That works whether the word made a list or not. Spotting the gap is a prerequisite to picking the assumption. pick assumption &rarr; spot gap Four that come up prerequisite A requirement, so it goes right. physics &rarr; calculus depends on A requirement, so it goes right. picnic &rarr; good weather will An outcome, so it goes right too. deadline passes &rarr; form locks the moment that A trigger, so it goes left. the moment it ships &rarr; billing starts Flipping a rule A rule and its flip are two sides of the same coin. Flipping is what the test is really checking, and the third step is where the points are. A and B &rarr; C NOT C &rarr; NOT A or NOT B Swap What was on the right goes on the left. Negate Positive becomes negative, negative becomes positive. Switch the connector Every and becomes an or, every or becomes an and. The two things you do with a rule Everything above gets the arrow onto the page. From there the test only ever asks for two things. A &rarr; B or C, plus A, plus NOT C The whole thing, in five steps 01 Find the word. One word tells you which side is the trigger and which side is required. 02 Do what it says. Left, right, negate left, or negate right. 03 Flip it when the question wants the other direction. Swap, negate, and switch every and to an or. 04 Chain it or trigger it. The Breakdown Where each of these gets taught properly, one part at a time. \\"}\\n{\\"id\\":\\"5aa2bb01-2103-4fab-b25d-3a26427498e5\\",\\"title\\":\\"Before you start: Conditional Logic (Easier) Set 4\\",\\"status\\":\\"assigned\\",\\"visible_to_student\\":true,\\"characters\\":4864,\\"body\\":\\" Day 1 &middot; Conditionals &middot; Note 1 of 2 If, then, and nothing else Read this before you open Conditional Logic (Easier) Set 4 . Four minutes, then go. What a conditional actually claims A conditional is a promise. It says: when this is true, that has to be true as well. That is the whole claim. It is not a claim about causes, about how often, or about what usually happens. It is a guarantee, and guarantees are easy to test. Every conditional has two halves doing two different jobs. sufficient &rarr; necessary The sufficient half is enough on its own. Have it, and the promise fires. The necessary half is what the promise buys you. It has to be there, but having it proves nothing on its own. How to spot which is which Certain words tell you the sufficient half is coming. Whatever follows them is the trigger: if &middot; when &middot; whenever &middot; all &middot; every &middot; any &middot; each So in \\\\\\"every senator voted,\\\\\\" being a senator is the trigger and voting is what it guarantees. Write the arrow before you look at a single answer choice. On these questions the diagram is not showing your work. It is the work. The one thing it never says The arrow points one way. It does not run backwards, and the test makes most of its money on people who let it. rain &rarr; the ground is wet Wet ground does not tell you it rained. Someone could have washed a car. The promise was only ever made in one direction, and an answer choice that walks it back the other way is wrong no matter how sensible it sounds. While you drill Find the indicator word first. It tells you which half is the trigger. Write the arrow before reading the choices. Every time, even when it feels obvious. When a choice feels right, check the direction it runs. That is where these go wrong. One habit for this week After you finish, go back through the ones you got wrong before you look at anything else, and work out what the right answer was without timing yourself. You have fifteen tests and fifty-five sections logged and no blind review on any of them. Until that changes, neither of us can tell whether a miss was the concept or the clock. Start here, on six questions. It is a small enough set to actually do it. \\"}\\n{\\"id\\":\\"0faaffff-fb5c-40c9-949b-72521bea662f\\",\\"title\\":\\"Before you start: Conditional Logic (Easier) Set 1\\",\\"status\\":\\"assigned\\",\\"visible_to_student\\":true,\\"characters\\":4510,\\"body\\":\\" Day 1 &middot; Conditionals &middot; Note 2 of 2 The same claim, said backwards Read this before you open Conditional Logic (Easier) Set 1 . It picks up exactly where the first set left off. There is exactly one legal way to flip it Set 4 taught you the arrow only runs one way. That is true, with one exception, and it is the single most useful move in this part of the test. You can reverse a conditional if you also negate both halves. Reverse and negate together, never one without the other. The result says the identical thing as the original. It is not a new fact. It is the same fact wearing different clothes, which is why the correct answer is so often hiding in it. rain &rarr; the ground is wet the ground is not wet &rarr; it did not rain Both sentences are one claim. If dry ground were possible after rain, the first promise was never true to begin with. Reversing without negating is the error. Negating without reversing is the same error from the other side. The two moves travel together. Two phrasings that trip almost everyone \\\\\\"Only if\\\\\\" points at the necessary half. It looks like \\\\\\"if\\\\\\" and behaves like its opposite. \\\\\\"You may enter only if you have a ticket\\\\\\" does not promise that a ticket gets you in. It promises that without one, you are not getting in. enter &rarr; ticket \\\\\\"Unless\\\\\\" means \\\\\\"if not.\\\\\\" Take whatever follows the word unless, negate it, and make that your trigger. \\\\\\"We will go unless it rains\\\\\\" becomes: if it does not rain, we go. no rain &rarr; we go Do not try to feel your way through these two. Translate them mechanically, on the page, and then reason about what you wrote. The intuition comes later and it comes from the reps. While you drill Diagram the stimulus, then write the flipped version underneath it before you read the choices. When you meet \\\\\\"only if\\\\\\" or \\\\\\"unless,\\\\\\" translate on paper. Do not translate in your head. If a choice reverses the arrow, check whether it negated both halves. If it did not, it is out, and you can stop reading it. This set steps up from Set 4, so expect it to feel harder. That is the design, not a signal that something has gone wrong. Blind review the misses before you check anything. \\"}\\n"}'}]

## Tool: custom_tool_call_output

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 2.0 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"78db27","wall_time_seconds":1.8587937,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":4860,"output":"Quantity statements tell us whether a claim applies to some members of a group, most of them, or all of them. Suppose a class has been assigned a case to read. “Some students read it,” “most students read it,” and “all students read it” are three different claims about that same class. We do not need an exact headcount to understand the difference.\\r\\n\\r\\nFor each sentence, ask: **What must be true? What could still be true?** The answer changes with the word we use.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Some guarantees at least one\\r\\n\\r\\nImagine a shelf with eight books. I tell you that **some of the books are hardcover**. How many hardcover books must there be?\\r\\n\\r\\nAt least **one**. There could be one, four, or all eight. “Some” rules out zero. It does not rule out “most” or “all.”\\r\\n\\r\\nThis is easy to miss because in ordinary conversation “some” often suggests “some, but not all.” On the LSAT, that extra phrase is not part of the statement. If all eight books are hardcover, it is still true that some are hardcover.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Try it:** “Some applicants received an interview.” Could every applicant have received one? Does the sentence tell us that anyone was denied an interview?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer**\\r\\n\\r\\nEvery applicant could have received an interview. The sentence does not tell us that anyone was denied one. “Some” guarantees at least one applicant received an interview, but it leaves open how many others did.\\r\\n\\r\\nWhenever you read **some**, replace it with **at least one** in your head. That tells you exactly what the sentence guarantees:\\r\\n\\r\\n> **Some of the books are hardcover.**\\r\\n>\\r\\n> **At least one of the books is hardcover.**\\r\\n\\r\\nNow focus on that one book. It belongs to both groups: it is a book, and it is hardcover. If we describe the same object from the hardcover side, we can say:\\r\\n\\r\\n> **Some hardcover items are books.**\\r\\n>\\r\\n> **At least one hardcover item is a book.**\\r\\n\\r\\nWe have not found a new object. We have described the same overlap in the other direction. That is why a **some** statement works both ways:\\r\\n\\r\\n```text\\r\\nBooks  ←some→  Hardcover\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nThe double arrow tells us that at least one thing belongs to both groups. It does not tell us that every book is hardcover or that every hardcover item is a book.\\r\\n\\r\\n### What would make “some” false?\\r\\n\\r\\nIf **some books are hardcover** is false, then **no books are hardcover**. There is not even one book in both groups. Notice the difference between **no books are hardcover** and **some books are not hardcover**. The second sentence still allows other books to be hardcover, so it does not negate “some.”\\r\\n\\r\\n## Most guarantees more than half\\r\\n\\r\\nNow I tell you that **most of the eight books are hardcover**. We need at least **five** hardcover books. Four would be exactly half, and half is not most. Five, six, seven, or eight could satisfy the sentence.\\r\\n\\r\\nNotice that all eight could still be hardcover. “Most” does not tell us that an exception exists.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Try it:** If most of the eight books are hardcover, must some of them be hardcover? Must any be paperback?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer**\\r\\n\\r\\nAt least five are hardcover, so **some are hardcover**. No paperback book is required. All eight could be hardcover.\\r\\n\\r\\nHere is where the direction of the statement matters. From “most books are hardcover,” we know that **some hardcover things are books**. We cannot say that **most hardcover things are books**. Five of our eight shelf books could be hardcover, while a library holds hundreds of other hardcover items. The first statement counts the eight books; the reversed statement counts a different group.\\r\\n\\r\\n```text\\r\\nBooks  ─most→  Hardcover\\r\\nHardcover  ←some→  Books\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nThe first arrow gives the majority claim. The second line is the overlap that the majority guarantees. When we read a most statement backward, the word becomes **some**.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Quick check:** A program has 100 applicants. Most speak Spanish. What is the smallest possible number who speak Spanish? What is the largest?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer**\\r\\n\\r\\n**51 to 100.** Fifty is only half. “Most” also permits all 100, so it does not establish an exception.\\r\\n\\r\\n### What would make “most” false?\\r\\n\\r\\n“Most of the eight books are hardcover” needs at least five hardcover books. Its negation permits **four or fewer**. Four is exactly half, so it already makes “most” false. The opposite of “most are hardcover” is **half or fewer are hardcover**, not “most are paperback.” Both kinds could make up exactly half.\\r\\n\\r\\n## All leaves no exception\\r\\n\\r\\nIf **all eight books are hardcover**, every book on the shelf is hardcover. One paperback on that shelf would make the statement false.\\r\\n\\r\\nAn all statement gives us a rule: **if it is one of these eight books, then it is hardcover**.\\r\\n\\r\\n```text\\r\\nOne of these books  →  Hardcover\\r\\nNot hardcover       →  Not one of these books\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nThe second line is the contrapositive. Both lines go with the same rule. We cannot turn the first line around and conclude that every hardcover thing is a book on this shelf.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Try it:** You pick up a paperback. What does the rule tell you? What if you pick up a hardcover book from another shelf?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer**\\r\\n\\r\\nThe paperback cannot be one of these eight books. A hardcover book from another shelf does not become one of these eight just because it is hardcover. Being hardcover is required, but it is not enough to identify one of these books.\\r\\n\\r\\nThere is a small qualification. “All A are B” gives a rule about A. By itself, it does not tell us that any A exist. When we already know the group exists, all also guarantees most and some.\\r\\n\\r\\n### What would make “all” false?\\r\\n\\r\\nOne exception is enough. “Not all eight books are hardcover” means **at least one of the eight is not hardcover**. The other seven could still be hardcover. This is the negation of the all claim. By contrast, “not hardcover → not one of these eight books” is its **contrapositive**: another way to state the original rule, not a way to deny it.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Put the three statements side by side\\r\\n\\r\\nThe same shelf can help us see the whole range.\\r\\n\\r\\nRead the implications in one direction:\\r\\n\\r\\n```text\\r\\nALL  →  MOST  →  SOME       (when the group exists)\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nIf all eight books are hardcover, then most are, and some are. If only “some” is given, all eight **could** be hardcover, but we cannot conclude that they **are**. Keep the distinction between a possible situation and a required one.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Carry a claim through an all rule\\r\\n\\r\\nNow we can combine statements. Suppose **some library books are mysteries** and **all mysteries are fiction**. We have at least one book that is a mystery. The all rule applies to every mystery, including that one. So **some library books are fiction**.\\r\\n\\r\\n```text\\r\\nLibrary books  ←some→  Mysteries  →  Fiction\\r\\nLibrary books  ←some→  Fiction\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nThe all arrow points **away from** the mystery group we reached. That is why the chain works. If the second sentence said only “most mysteries are fiction,” the mystery books we found could be among the exceptions.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Quick check:** Some archive documents are letters. All letters are handwritten. What follows?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer**\\r\\n\\r\\n**Some archive documents are handwritten.** Take a document guaranteed to be a letter. The all rule applies to that document.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe same reasoning preserves the first quantity word:\\r\\n\\r\\n| First statement | Rule | What follows |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n| Some library books are mysteries. | All mysteries are fiction. | Some library books are fiction. |\\r\\n| Most library books are mysteries. | All mysteries are fiction. | Most library books are fiction. |\\r\\n| All library books are mysteries. | All mysteries are fiction. | All library books are fiction. |\\r\\n\\r\\n**Try it:** “Most library books are mysteries. All fiction is mystery.” Can we conclude that most library books are fiction?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer**\\r\\n\\r\\nNo. The all arrow goes from fiction to mystery. We cannot start at mystery and travel backward to fiction.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Two majorities of the same group must meet\\r\\n\\r\\nSuppose a club has ten members. **Most members study law**, and **most members work part time**. Each “most” needs at least six of the ten members. Those two sets cannot fit inside the club without sharing at least two people.\\r\\n\\r\\n**What is given?** More than half of the *same ten members* study law. More than half of those same ten work part time. The claims count club members, not all law students or all part-time workers everywhere.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Why must they meet?** Each group needs at least six members. If the groups were separate, we would need twelve different club members. There are only ten. At least two members must be in both groups.\\r\\n\\r\\n**What follows?** **Some law students work part time.** We can reverse that some claim: **some part-time workers study law**. Both sentences describe the same overlapping members. We know at least one exists; we do not know that most law students or most part-time workers have the other trait.\\r\\n\\r\\n```text\\r\\nGiven:       Most club members study law.\\r\\n             Most club members work part time.\\r\\nMust follow: Some law students work part time.\\r\\nWhy:         Both majorities count the same club members.\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n**Quick check:** A club has 25 members. Most are lawyers, and most play an instrument. What is the smallest possible number who are both lawyers and play an instrument?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer**\\r\\n\\r\\n**One.** Each majority needs at least 13 members. If none overlapped, those groups would require 26 different people, but the club has only 25. All 25 could also have both traits.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Try it:** If most club members study law and most club members work part time, could the two groups of members be completely separate?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer**\\r\\n\\r\\nNo. Two separate majorities would require more members than the club has. At least one member must have both traits.\\r\\n\\r\\n### When all describes that same group\\r\\n\\r\\nThere is another way to guarantee an overlap. Suppose **most club members study law**, and **all club members work part time**. We already know there are law students in the club. The all rule tells us that *each one of those members* also works part time. So **some law students work part time**.\\r\\n\\r\\n```text\\r\\nGiven:       Most club members study law.\\r\\n             All club members work part time.\\r\\nMust follow: Some law students work part time.\\r\\nWhy:         The law students already found are club members,\\r\\n             so the all rule applies to each of them.\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nWe can make the same move with **some**. If **some club members study law** and **all club members work part time**, take the member guaranteed by “some.” That person has both traits. Again, **some law students work part time**. The sentences can be stated in the other order: **all club members work part time; some club members study law**. The same person still proves the overlap.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Try it:** All A are B. All A are C. Must some B be C?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer**\\r\\n\\r\\nNot yet. The two all rules tell us what is true **if** something is A, but neither tells us that any A exists. Add “at least one A exists,” and the overlap follows.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Two majorities can also compare group sizes\\r\\n\\r\\nHere is a different comparison. **Most debate team members are seniors**, but **most seniors are not on the debate team**. The seniors on the team are more than half of the team and less than half of the seniors. The same overlap takes up a larger share of the team than of the senior class. Therefore, **there are more seniors than debate team members**.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe numbers in the picture are one possible case. The conclusion follows from the two majority claims, not from those particular numbers.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Similar chains can leave a gap\\r\\n\\r\\nTwo some statements need not describe the same person or thing:\\r\\n\\r\\n```text\\r\\nSome volunteers speak Spanish.\\r\\nSome Spanish speakers are trained.\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nThe trained Spanish speakers could all be people who do not volunteer. We cannot conclude that any volunteers are trained.\\r\\n\\r\\nReplacing the second “some” with “most” does not fix the gap. The Spanish-speaking volunteers could sit in the untrained minority. Even two most statements that count different groups do not automatically produce a new majority claim.\\r\\n\\r\\nBefore drawing a conclusion, ask: **Am I carrying a known group through an all rule? Or do two majorities count the same group?** If neither is true, check whether the groups could miss each other.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Quick check:** A club has 13 members. Most are women, and some are dancers. How many female dancers are guaranteed? How many are possible?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer**\\r\\n\\r\\n**Zero are guaranteed; as many as 13 are possible.** Seven women and one dancer meet the statements even if that dancer is one of the six men. Both traits could also belong to every member. A most claim and a some claim about the same group do not force the two traits to meet.\\r\\n\\r\\n## The moves to keep\\r\\n\\r\\n| What you have | What you may conclude |\\r\\n|---|---|\\r\\n| Some A are B | Some B are A |\\r\\n| Most A are B | Some A are B, and some B are A |\\r\\n| All A are B | A → B, and not B → not A |\\r\\n| Some/most/all A are B; all B are C | The same first quantity of A are C |\\r\\n| Most A are B; most A are C | Some B are C |\\r\\n| Most A are B; all A are C | Some B are C |\\r\\n| Some A are B; all A are C | Some B are C |\\r\\n\\r\\nTwo **all** claims about A guarantee that every A has both traits. They guarantee **some B are C** only when we also know that at least one A exists.\\r\\n\\r\\nUse the sentence behind each arrow to check what it guarantees.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Nearby words need a closer look\\r\\n\\r\\nThe words around a quantity statement can look familiar without making the same promise. Sort them by **what they guarantee**, then check the sentence in front of you:\\r\\n\\r\\n| Word or phrase | What it guarantees |\\r\\n|---|---|\\r\\n| **Some**, **at least one**, **one or more** | One or more members fit. All may fit too. |\\r\\n| **A few**, **several**, **many** | A positive number in ordinary use, but no universal cutoff. They imply at least one. |\\r\\n| **Most**, **more than half**, **a majority of** | Strictly more than half of the named group fit. |\\r\\n| **All**, **every**, **each**, **without exception** | Every member of the named group fits. The group may still need a separate existence premise. |\\r\\n| **Few**, **not many**, **not often** | A small number or low frequency in context. No exact numerical boundary follows. |\\r\\n| **No**, **none**, **zero** | Not a single member fits. |\\r\\n| **Not all** | At least one member does **not** fit. |\\r\\n\\r\\n**Few** is worth separating from **a few**. “A few books are hardcover” affirms that at least some are. “Few books are hardcover” stresses how small the number is. It often suggests some in ordinary speech, but it does not set a dependable numerical minimum. “Not many” can include zero. A hundred menu items could make five feel like few; in a menu of six, five would not feel like few. Do not quietly replace any of these phrases with a fixed number.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Most** is different: it crosses a precise line, **more than half**. Words such as **the majority** plainly express that idea. Words such as **usually**, **typically**, **generally**, **commonly**, **frequently**, **often**, **likely**, and **probably** can suggest prevalence in context, but pay attention to what the sentence actually counts. “Probably” about one event, for example, is a likelihood claim, not a headcount of a group.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Not all** *does* give us a precise minimum: at least one exception. “Not all club members are lawyers” means **at least one club member is not a lawyer**. It does not tell us whether the other members are lawyers.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Quick check:** A menu lists 100 beers. “Many are Belgian,” “few are Belgian,” and “not many are Belgian” are three claims. Can you assign each one an exact minimum and maximum? What does “not all are Belgian” guarantee?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer**\\r\\n\\r\\nThe first three do **not** have fixed numerical boundaries. “Many” suggests more than an isolated example, while “few” and “not many” suggest a small number relative to the context. None alone licenses a specific cutoff such as five or ten. “Not all” is exact about one thing: **at least one beer is not Belgian**.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Pop quiz: decide whether the overlap follows\\r\\n\\r\\nFor each pair, decide whether it guarantees **some A are C**. Draw a witness or explain where the groups could miss each other before reading the answers.\\r\\n\\r\\n1. Some A are B. All B are C.\\r\\n2. Some A are B. Most A are C.\\r\\n3. All A are B. Some B are C.\\r\\n4. Some A are B. Most B are C.\\r\\n5. Some A are B. Some B are C.\\r\\n6. Most A are B. Most A are C.\\r\\n7. All A are B. All A are C.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer**\\r\\n\\r\\n1. **Yes.** The A that is B must also be C because every B is C.\\r\\n2. **No.** The A that is B could be outside the majority of A that is C.\\r\\n3. **No.** The B that is C could be outside A. The all rule runs from A to B, not back from B to A.\\r\\n4. **No.** The known A that is B could be among the B that are not C.\\r\\n5. **No.** The B in the first statement could differ from the B in the second.\\r\\n6. **Yes.** Two majorities of the same A group must share at least one member, who is both B and C.\\r\\n7. **Not from these statements alone.** Every A would be both B and C, but the statements do not establish that an A exists. If at least one A exists, the answer becomes yes.\\r\\n\\r\\n“No” here means **no guaranteed A–C overlap**. The premises may still imply a different, smaller fact. For example, statement 3\'s “some B are C” is already given.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Short drill: diagram, combine, decide\\r\\n\\r\\nHere are five statements about one volunteer program. **Volunteer**, **Spanish speaker**, **trained person**, **weekend worker**, and **student** name groups of people. Treat each sentence as true.\\r\\n\\r\\n1. Some volunteers speak Spanish.\\r\\n2. All Spanish speakers are trained.\\r\\n3. Most volunteers work weekends.\\r\\n4. Most volunteers are students.\\r\\n5. Most weekend workers are trained.\\r\\n\\r\\nFirst, diagram each statement. Then answer these without looking below:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. Which two statements prove that **some volunteers are trained**?\\r\\n2. Which two statements prove that **some students work weekends**?\\r\\n3. Do statements 3 and 5 prove that **most volunteers are trained**? Explain the gap or draw a counterexample.\\r\\n4. Can you write either valid **some** conclusion in the reverse order?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer**\\r\\n\\r\\n```text\\r\\n1. Volunteers       ←some→  Spanish speakers\\r\\n2. Spanish speakers →       Trained people\\r\\n3. Volunteers       ─most→  Weekend workers\\r\\n4. Volunteers       ─most→  Students\\r\\n5. Weekend workers  ─most→  Trained people\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n**1. Statements 1 and 2.** At least one volunteer speaks Spanish. Every Spanish speaker is trained. That volunteer is trained, so **some volunteers are trained**.\\r\\n\\r\\n**2. Statements 3 and 4.** Both majorities count the volunteers. The weekend workers and students must share at least one volunteer. So **some students work weekends**.\\r\\n\\r\\n**3. No.** Statement 3 says most volunteers work weekends. Statement 5 counts all weekend workers, a different group. The volunteers who work weekends could be among the untrained weekend workers. Nothing requires most volunteers to be trained.\\r\\n\\r\\n**4. Yes.** A some statement describes an overlap in either direction: **some trained people are volunteers**, and **some weekend workers are students**.\\r\\n\\r\\n"}'}]

## Tool: exec

text(await tools.exec_command({cmd:"node --input-type=module -e \"import fs from 'node:fs';import {projectPath} from './system/scripts/project-paths.mjs';const read=f=>Object.fromEntries(fs.readFileSync(f,'utf8').split(/\\r?\\n/).filter(l=>l.trim()&&!l.trim().startsWith('#')&&l.includes('=')).map(l=>{const i=l.indexOf('=');return [l.slice(0,i).trim(),l.slice(i+1).trim().replace(/^[\\x22']|[\\x22']$/g,'')]}));const e={...read(projectPath('lsatu','.env')),...read('.env')},b=e.VITE_SUPABASE_URL,k=e.VITE_SUPABASE_PUBLISHABLE_KEY;const t=await(await fetch(b+'/auth/v1/token?grant_type=password',{method:'POST',headers:{apikey:[REDACTED],'content-type':'application/json'},body:JSON.stringify({email:e.LSATPREP_ADMIN_EMAIL,password:[REDACTED]})})).json();if(!t.access_token)throw Error('Auth failed');const r=await fetch(b+'/rest/v1/classroom_items?id=eq.c9228402-da0e-4411-984e-17831a798e5c&select=id,title,status,visible_to_student,content_html',{headers:{apikey:[REDACTED],authorization:[REDACTED]}});if(!r.ok)throw Error('Read failed');for(const row of await r.json()){const h=row.content_html||'';console.log(JSON.stringify({...row,content_html:undefined,characters:h.length,body:h.replace(/<(style|script)[^>]*>[\\s\\S]*?<\\/\\1>/g,'').replace(/<[^>]+>/g,' ').replace(/\\s+/g,' ')}));}\"","max_output_tokens":9000}));


## Tool: custom_tool_call_output

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 3.4 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"16f8eb","wall_time_seconds":3.2056927,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":4634,"output":"{\\"id\\":\\"c9228402-da0e-4411-984e-17831a798e5c\\",\\"title\\":\\"Some, Most, and All\\",\\"status\\":\\"assigned\\",\\"visible_to_student\\":true,\\"characters\\":447579,\\"body\\":\\" Some, Most, and All | LSAT U visual study September 29, 2026 | Note Some, Most, and All Quantity statements tell us whether a claim applies to some members of a group, most of them, or all of them. Suppose a class has been assigned a case to read. These are three different claims about that same class: Some students read it. Most students read it. All students read it. We do not need an exact headcount to understand the difference. What must be true? What could still be true? The answer changes with the word we use. A little experiment 1 of 8 books are hardcover Move the count. Watch which statements become true. Some 1–8 Most 5–8 All 8 Some Most All Inferences Practice Drill 01 Some guarantees at least one Imagine a shelf with eight books. I tell you that some of the books are hardcover . How many hardcover books must there be? At least one . There could be one, four, or all eight. “Some” rules out zero. It does not rule out “most” or “all.” This is easy to miss because in ordinary conversation “some” often suggests “some, but not all.” On the LSAT, that extra phrase is not part of the statement. If all eight books are hardcover, it is still true that some are hardcover. Try it 01 “Some applicants received an interview.” Could every applicant have received one? Does the sentence tell us that anyone was denied an interview? Your answer Check my answer ↗ Every applicant could have received an interview. The sentence does not tell us that anyone was denied one. “Some” guarantees at least one applicant received an interview, but it leaves open how many others did. Edit my answer Whenever you read some , replace it with at least one in your head. That tells you exactly what the sentence guarantees: Some of the books are hardcover. At least one of the books is hardcover. Now focus on that one book. It belongs to both groups: it is a book, and it is hardcover. If we describe the same object from the hardcover side, we can say: Some hardcover items are books. At least one hardcover item is a book. We have not found a new object. We have described the same overlap in the other direction. That is why a some statement works both ways: Books ←some→ Hardcover The double arrow tells us that at least one thing belongs to both groups. It does not tell us that every book is hardcover or that every hardcover item is a book. What would make “some” false? If some books are hardcover is false, then no books are hardcover . There is not even one book in both groups. Notice the difference between no books are hardcover and some books are not hardcover . The second sentence still allows other books to be hardcover, so it does not negate “some.” 02 Most guarantees more than half Now I tell you that most of the eight books are hardcover . We need at least five hardcover books. Four would be exactly half, and half is not most. Five, six, seven, or eight could satisfy the sentence. Notice that all eight could still be hardcover. “Most” does not tell us that an exception exists. Try it 02 If most of the eight books are hardcover, must some of them be hardcover? Must any be paperback? Your answer Check my answer ↗ At least five are hardcover, so some are hardcover . No paperback book is required. All eight could be hardcover. Edit my answer Here is where the direction of the statement matters. From “most books are hardcover,” we know that some hardcover things are books . We cannot say that most hardcover things are books . Five of our eight shelf books could be hardcover, while a library holds hundreds of other hardcover items. The first statement counts the eight books; the reversed statement counts a different group. Books ─most→ Hardcover Hardcover ←some→ Books The first arrow gives the majority claim. The second line is the overlap that the majority guarantees. When we read a most statement backward, the word becomes some . Quick check 03 A program has 100 applicants. Most speak Spanish. What is the smallest possible number who speak Spanish? What is the largest? Your answer Check my answer ↗ 51 to 100. Fifty is only half. “Most” also permits all 100, so it does not establish an exception. Edit my answer What would make “most” false? “Most of the eight books are hardcover” needs at least five hardcover books. Its negation permits four or fewer . Four is exactly half, so it already makes “most” false. The opposite of “most are hardcover” is half or fewer are hardcover , not “most are paperback.” Both kinds could make up exactly half. 03 All leaves no exception If all eight books are hardcover , every book on the shelf is hardcover. One paperback on that shelf would make the statement false. An all statement gives us a rule: if it is one of these eight books, then it is hardcover . One of these books → Hardcover Not hardcover → Not one of these books The second line is the contrapositive. Both lines go with the same rule. We cannot turn the first line around and conclude that every hardcover thing is a book on this shelf. Try it 04 You pick up a paperback. What does the rule tell you? What if you pick up a hardcover book from another shelf? Your answer Check my answer ↗ The paperback cannot be one of these eight books. A hardcover book from another shelf does not become one of these eight just because it is hardcover. Being hardcover is required, but it is not enough to identify one of these books. Edit my answer There is a small qualification. “All A are B” gives a rule about A. By itself, it does not tell us that any A exist. When we already know the group exists, all also guarantees most and some. What would make “all” false? One exception is enough. “Not all eight books are hardcover” means at least one of the eight is not hardcover . The other seven could still be hardcover. This is the negation of the all claim. By contrast, “not hardcover → not one of these eight books” is its contrapositive : another way to state the original rule, not a way to deny it. 04 Put the three statements side by side The same shelf can help us see the whole range. Read the implications in one direction: ALL → MOST → SOME (when the group exists) If all eight books are hardcover, then most are, and some are. If only “some” is given, all eight could be hardcover, but we cannot conclude that they are . Keep the distinction between a possible situation and a required one. 05 Carry a claim through an all rule Now we can combine statements. Suppose some library books are mysteries and all mysteries are fiction . We have at least one book that is a mystery. The all rule applies to every mystery, including that one. So some library books are fiction . Library books ←some→ Mysteries → Fiction Library books ←some→ Fiction The all arrow points away from the mystery group we reached. That is why the chain works. If the second sentence said only “most mysteries are fiction,” the mystery books we found could be among the exceptions. Quick check 05 Some archive documents are letters. All letters are handwritten. What follows? Your answer Check my answer ↗ Some archive documents are handwritten. Take a document guaranteed to be a letter. The all rule applies to that document. Edit my answer The same reasoning preserves the first quantity word: First statement Rule What follows Some library books are mysteries. All mysteries are fiction. Some library books are fiction. Most library books are mysteries. All mysteries are fiction. Most library books are fiction. All library books are mysteries. All mysteries are fiction. All library books are fiction. Try it 06 “Most library books are mysteries. All fiction is mystery.” Can we conclude that most library books are fiction? Your answer Check my answer ↗ No. The all arrow goes from fiction to mystery. We cannot start at mystery and travel backward to fiction. Edit my answer 06 Two majorities of the same group must meet Suppose a club has ten members. Most members study law , and most members work part time . Each “most” needs at least six of the ten members. Those two sets cannot fit inside the club without sharing at least two people. Try to separate the majorities. Six of ten club members study law. Six work part time. Rearrange the workers and watch what cannot change. 01 02 03 04 05 06 07 08 09 10 Studies law Works part time Both 2 members have both traits. Even the smallest possible overlap is not zero. Rearrange the workers ↻ The universe is the ten club members. The shared middle cannot be empty. All ten club members Study law Work part time 4 2 4 Neither: 0 The circle areas are schematic. The numbers count members and change when you rearrange them. What is given? More than half of the same ten members study law. More than half of those same ten work part time. The claims count club members, not all law students or all part-time workers everywhere. Why must they meet? Each group needs at least six members. If the groups were separate, we would need twelve different club members. There are only ten. At least two members must be in both groups. What follows? Some law students work part time. We can reverse that some claim: some part-time workers study law . Both sentences describe the same overlapping members. We know at least one exists; we do not know that most law students or most part-time workers have the other trait. Given: Most club members study law. Most club members work part time. Must follow: Some law students work part time. Why: Both majorities count the same club members. Quick check 07 A club has 25 members. Most are lawyers, and most play an instrument. What is the smallest possible number who are both lawyers and play an instrument? Your answer Check my answer ↗ One. Each majority needs at least 13 members. If none overlapped, those groups would require 26 different people, but the club has only 25. All 25 could also have both traits. Edit my answer Try it 08 If most club members study law and most club members work part time, could the two groups of members be completely separate? Your answer Check my answer ↗ No. Two separate majorities would require more members than the club has. At least one member must have both traits. Edit my answer When all describes that same group There is another way to guarantee an overlap. Suppose most club members study law , and all club members work part time . We already know there are law students in the club. The all rule tells us that each one of those members also works part time. So some law students work part time . Given: Most club members study law. All club members work part time. Must follow: Some law students work part time. Why: The law students already found are club members, so the all rule applies to each of them. We can make the same move with some . If some club members study law and all club members work part time , take the member guaranteed by “some.” That person has both traits. Again, some law students work part time . The sentences can be stated in the other order: all club members work part time; some club members study law . The same person still proves the overlap. Try it 09 All A are B. All A are C. Must some B be C? Your answer Check my answer ↗ Not yet. The two all rules tell us what is true if something is A, but neither tells us that any A exists. Add “at least one A exists,” and the overlap follows. Edit my answer 07 Two majorities can also compare group sizes Here is a different comparison. Most debate team members are seniors , but most seniors are not on the debate team . The seniors on the team are more than half of the team and less than half of the seniors. The same overlap takes up a larger share of the team than of the senior class. Therefore, there are more seniors than debate team members . The numbers in the picture are one possible case. The conclusion follows from the two majority claims, not from those particular numbers. 08 Similar chains can leave a gap Two some statements need not describe the same person or thing: Some volunteers speak Spanish. Some Spanish speakers are trained. The trained Spanish speakers could all be people who do not volunteer. We cannot conclude that any volunteers are trained. Replacing the second “some” with “most” does not fix the gap. The Spanish-speaking volunteers could sit in the untrained minority. Even two most statements that count different groups do not automatically produce a new majority claim. Before drawing a conclusion, ask: Am I carrying a known group through an all rule? Or do two majorities count the same group? If neither is true, check whether the groups could miss each other. Quick check 10 A club has 13 members. Most are women, and some are dancers. How many female dancers are guaranteed? How many are possible? Your answer Check my answer ↗ Zero are guaranteed; as many as 13 are possible. Seven women and one dancer meet the statements even if that dancer is one of the six men. Both traits could also belong to every member. A most claim and a some claim about the same group do not force the two traits to meet. Edit my answer 09 The moves to keep What you have What you may conclude Some A are B Some B are A Most A are B Some A are B, and some B are A All A are B A → B, and not B → not A Some/most/all A are B; all B are C The same first quantity of A are C Most A are B; most A are C Some B are C Most A are B; all A are C Some B are C Some A are B; all A are C Some B are C Two all claims about A guarantee that every A has both traits. They guarantee some B are C only when we also know that at least one A exists. Use the sentence behind each arrow to check what it guarantees. 10 Nearby words need a closer look The words around a quantity statement can look familiar without making the same promise. Sort them by what they guarantee , then check the sentence in front of you: Word or phrase What it guarantees Some , at least one , one or more One or more members fit. All may fit too. A few , several , many A positive number in ordinary use, but no universal cutoff. They imply at least one. Most , more than half , a majority of Strictly more than half of the named group fit. All , every , each , without exception Every member of the named group fits. The group may still need a separate existence premise. Few , not many , not often A small number or low frequency in context. No exact numerical boundary follows. No , none , zero Not a single member fits. Not all At least one member does not fit. Few is worth separating from a few . “A few books are hardcover” affirms that at least some are. “Few books are hardcover” stresses how small the number is. It often suggests some in ordinary speech, but it does not set a dependable numerical minimum. “Not many” can include zero. A hundred menu items could make five feel like few; in a menu of six, five would not feel like few. Do not quietly replace any of these phrases with a fixed number. Most is different: it crosses a precise line, more than half . Words such as the majority plainly express that idea. Words such as usually , typically , generally , commonly , frequently , often , likely , and probably can suggest prevalence in context, but pay attention to what the sentence actually counts. “Probably” about one event, for example, is a likelihood claim, not a headcount of a group. Not all does give us a precise minimum: at least one exception. “Not all club members are lawyers” means at least one club member is not a lawyer . It does not tell us whether the other members are lawyers. Quick check 11 A menu lists 100 beers. “Many are Belgian,” “few are Belgian,” and “not many are Belgian” are three claims. Can you assign each one an exact minimum and maximum? What does “not all are Belgian” guarantee? Your answer Check my answer ↗ The first three do not have fixed numerical boundaries. “Many” suggests more than an isolated example, while “few” and “not many” suggest a small number relative to the context. None alone licenses a specific cutoff such as five or ten. “Not all” is exact about one thing: at least one beer is not Belgian . Edit my answer Practice 01 / 07 Can you prove the overlap? Seven short answers. Make a call, explain it, then compare your reasoning. Given Some A are B. All B are C. A C Must some A be C? Start with Yes or No , then tell me why. Check my answer ↗ Compare your explanation with this one. Did you identify the same person or the same gap? Next question → Practice complete You checked each conclusion against a witness or a possible gap. Review any answer where your reason differed, even if your yes or no matched. Try the seven again ↺ 11 Short drill: diagram, combine, decide Here are five statements about one volunteer program. Volunteer , Spanish speaker , trained person , weekend worker , and student name groups of people. Treat each sentence as true. Some volunteers speak Spanish. All Spanish speakers are trained. Most volunteers work weekends. Most volunteers are students. Most weekend workers are trained. Diagram the five statements for yourself. Then work through these four questions. Your answer stays on the page while you compare it with the worked reason. Question 1 01 / 04 Which two statements prove that some volunteers are trained? Your answer Check my answer ↗ Statements 1 and 2. At least one volunteer speaks Spanish. Every Spanish speaker is trained. That volunteer is trained. Edit my answer Question 2 02 / 04 Which two statements prove that some students work weekends? Your answer Check my answer ↗ Statements 3 and 4. Both majorities count volunteers, so the students and weekend workers must share at least one volunteer. Edit my answer Question 3 03 / 04 Do statements 3 and 5 prove that most volunteers are trained? Explain. Your answer Check my answer ↗ No. Statement 3 counts volunteers; statement 5 counts all weekend workers. The volunteer weekend workers could be in the untrained minority of weekend workers. Edit my answer Question 4 04 / 04 Can either valid some conclusion be reversed? Give an example. Your answer Check my answer ↗ Yes. Some trained people are volunteers. Some weekend workers are students. Each sentence describes the same overlap in reverse. Edit my answer \\"}\\n"}'}]

Asia’s live notes correspond to the complete local HTML files in `students/asia/`. Her four lists total **44 expressions**, not 49: 19 left, 16 right, four negate-left, five negate-right. Exact inventory and HTML anchors sent to the content worker.

Substantive additions supported by the actual assigned notes:

- Broader conditional language coverage, including guarantee/result expressions, requirement synonyms, and unfamiliar phrasings.
- `otherwise` contrasted with a genuinely two-direction rule.
- Intermediate chain conclusions, whole-chain contraposition, and compound-result elimination.
- Introductory written translation before answer choices, with mechanical `only if`/`unless` practice.
- Blind review before explanation/checking to separate conceptual errors from timing.
- Diagramming when notation helps, after foundational written practice.
- Charles’s quantity lesson explicitly compares group sizes from opposing majority claims. That topic is absent from the existing four quantifier units.
- Charles’s teaching interface uses a movable count and rearrangeable majority overlap to expose what remains guaranteed. Existing textual questions cover the core logic but lack that manipulable witness experience.

Charles’s quantity lesson already carefully teaches some permitting all, most permitting all, denominators, exact negations, existence limits, same-group overlaps, partial-chain failures, and context-dependent “few/many.” Keep it in the later quantity phase.

Source defects must not be copied:

- Asia’s fixed “few = most not” claim conflicts with Charles’s newer, context-sensitive treatment.
- Word-only classification oversimplifies guarantees/implies/ensures and contextual “signals,” “not,” or “except.”
- Some Asia/Yahunathan examples shorten permission into actual entry; preserve `may enter`.
- Yahunathan’s “same fact” phrasing for contraposition should say the same conditional relationship.
- Personalized historical performance remarks are student-specific and should not enter the general course.

No new source files, edits, assignments, or other live writes.
